(see parameter setup
k
in §
4.3
).

By Lemma
4.9
,
Extract
(E,k)
is a contraction algorithm.
If all vertices from a component in the original graph contract to the same vertices, i.e., all these vertices are in the same flat tree in the labeled digraph, then the computation for this component is done and the algorithm can ignore all these vertices in the later execution.
If a root
v
still has an adjacent edge to another root, then the computation for the component of
v
is not done yet, and we call
v

active
.
The
current graph
is defined to be the graph induced on active roots (using the current edges altered by the algorithm).

At the end of the algorithm, the vertices in the current graph (active roots in the labeled digraph) consist of three parts:
(i) the active roots in
V^{\prime}
,
(ii) the active roots in
V(E^{\prime})
, and
(iii) the active roots not in
V^{\prime}\cup V(E^{\prime})
.
In the following, we bound each of them.

Lemma 4.17
.

At the end of
Extract
(E,k)
, the number of active roots in
V^{\prime}
is
0.999^{k}1000n
w.p.
1-k^{2}n^{-8}
.

Proof.

By Definition
4.11
,
V^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{k}V_{i}
.
By Lemma
4.14
,
|V_{i}|\leq 0.999^{k+i}n
for all
i\in[0,k]
w.p.
1-k^{2}n^{-8}
. The lemma follows immediately.
∎

Lemma 4.18
.

At the end of
Extract
(E,k)
, the number of active roots in
V(E^{\prime})
is
0.999^{k}n
w.p.
1-kn^{-8}
.

Proof.

Consider running
Extract
(E,k)
before its Step
4
.
By Definition
4.11
,
V(E^{\prime})=V(E_{k+1})=V^{\prime}_{k+1}
where
E_{k+1}
is the edge set
E^{\prime}
at the end of round
k
.
The proof of Lemma
4.12
implies that
|V^{\prime}_{k+1}|\leq 0.999^{k+1}n
w.p.
1-(k+1)n^{-9}
.
It is easy to verify that if a flat tree does not contain a vertex from
V^{\prime}
then it is unchanged in the
Reverse
(V^{\prime},E)
in Step
4
;
otherwise the flat tree becomes a flat tree rooted at a vertex
u\in V^{\prime}
and all adjacent edges of this tree are adjacent to
u
after
Reverse
(V^{\prime},E)
.
Therefore the number of active roots in
V(E^{\prime})
cannot increase, giving the lemma.
∎

Note that the above two lemmas bound the number of active roots by the number of vertices, which might not be tight but suffices for our proof.

Lemma 4.19
.

At the end of
Extract
(E,k)
, there is no active root in
V\backslash(V^{\prime}\cup V(E^{\prime}))
, where
V
is the vertex set in the input graph.

Proof.

Consider running
Extract
(E,k)
before its Step
4
.
Observe that any edge in the graph at this point is either in
E^{\prime}
or has both ends in
V^{\prime}
.
So, for any root
u\in V\backslash(V^{\prime}\cup V(E^{\prime}))
,
u
must have no adjacent edges.
If the flat tree rooted at
u
does not contain a vertex
v\in V^{\prime}
, then this tree must contain all vertices in the component of
u
in the input graph. This is because we never delete an edge (in Step
2
) unless it is between vertices in
V^{\prime}
.
Therefore, the computation for this component is done and
u
is not an active root.

Otherwise, if the flat tree rooted at
u
does contain a vertex from
V^{\prime}
, then
in the
Reverse
(V^{\prime},E)
(Step
4
), suppose
v\in V^{\prime}
and
v
((arbitrarily)) wins the concurrent writing
v.p.p=v
in Step
1
.
After this, we have
u.p=v
; after the following
v.p=v.p.p
in Step
1
, we have
v.p=v
, giving a tree rooted at
v
with height at most
2
.
Then, Step
2
makes this tree flat and
u
is no longer a root.
∎

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
k=1000\log\log\log n
.

Extract
(E,k)
runs on an ARBITRARY CRCW PRAM in
O(m)+\overline{O}(n)
work and
O((\log\log\log n)^{2}\log^{*}n)
time, and reduces the number of vertices in the current graph to
n/\log\log n
w.p.
1-n^{-4}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.16
,
Extract
(E,k)
runs in
O(m)+\overline{O}(n)
work and
O((\log\log\log n)^{2}\log^{*}n)
time w.p.
1-n^{-5}
.
By Lemma
4.17
, Lemma
4.18
, Lemma
4.19
, and the discussion before them, the number of vertices in the current graph is at most

1001n\cdot 0.999^{1000\log\log\log n}\leq n/\log\log n

w.p.
1-n^{-7}
. The lemma immediately follows from a union bound.
∎

4.3
A
\poly(\log n)
-Shrink Algorithm

In this section, we give an algorithm that runs in
O(\log\log n)
time and
O(m)+\overline{O}(n)
work and contracts the graph to
n/\poly(\log n)
vertices w.h.p.

Reduce
(V,E,k)
:

1.

Extract
(E,1000\log\log\log n)
.

2.

V^{\prime}=\textsc{Filter}(E,k)
.

3.

For each
v\in V
do
v.p=v.p.p
.
Alter
(E)
.

4.

Edge set
E^{\prime}=\{(u,v)\mid u\notin V^{\prime}\text{ or }v\notin V^{\prime}\}
.

5.

For
round

i
from
0
to
k
:
Matching
(E^{\prime})
, for each
v\in V
do
v.p=v.p.p
,
Alter
(E^{\prime})
.

6.

Reverse
(V^{\prime},E)
.

Lemma 4.21
.

If at the beginning of an execution of
Reduce
(V,E,k)
, both ends of any edge in
E
are roots and all trees in the labeled digraph have height
0
, then at the end of that execution both ends of any edge in
E
are still roots and all trees are flat.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.9
, the statement holds after Step
1
.
By Lemma
4.7
, after Step
2
, all trees have height at most
2
.
In Step
3
, all trees become flat and the
Alter
(E)
makes all edges adjacent to roots.
By an induction on round
i
from
0
to
k
in Step
5
and Lemma
4.5
, all trees have height at most
2
after
Matching
(E^{\prime})
, and become flat at the end of that round and all edges are adjacent to roots.
Using the same proof for
Reverse
as in Lemma
4.9
, the statements holds after Step
6
.
∎

Next, we prove that the algorithm runs in
O(m)+\overline{O}(n)
work.
We could not use the implementation for
Filter
(E,k)
in Lemma
4.10
, because here we want
O(k)=O(\log\log n)
running time instead of
O(k\log^{*}n)
time.
Observe that the number of vertices in current graph is reduced, so we can afford more work proportional to the number of vertices in pursuit for an improved running time.

Lemma 4.22
.

Filter
(E,k)
can be implemented on an ARBITRARY CRCW PRAM in
O(k)
time and
O(|E|+k\cdot|V(E)|)
work.

Proof.

In Step
1
, initially each edge knows itself to be in
E
.
In each round, the edge notifies itself to be not in
E
w.p.
10^{-4}
, and only the edge corresponding to a non-deleted edge in
E
performs work in
Matching
(E)
,
Alter
(E)
, and edge deletion.
Combining with Lemma
4.3
, round
j
takes
O(1)
time and
O(|E_{j}|)
work where
E_{j}
is the edge set
E
at the beginning of round
j
.
By a Chernoff bound,
\Pr[|E_{j}|\leq 0.99999|E_{j-1}|]
w.p.
1-n^{-9}
for each
j\in[k]
.
By a union bound,
|E_{j}|\leq 0.99999^{j}|E_{0}|
w.p.
1-n^{-8}
for all
j\in[k]
.
As a result, Step
1
takes
O(k)
time and
O(|E|)
work w.p.
1-n^{-8}
.

To implement Step
2
, each edge processor corresponding to edge
(u,v)
in each round
j
of Step
1
also stores the ends
u
(resp.
v
) into a cell indexed by vertex
u
(resp.
v
) and
j
in the public RAM
6
6

6

For example, the cell with index
j\cdot|A|+\textsf{id}(u)
where
A
is the array storing all vertices in
V(E)
and
|A|\leq 2|V(E)|
by approximate compaction (see the proof of Lemma
4.23
) and
\textsf{id}(u)
is the id of the processor corresponding to vertex
u
.

if vertex
u
(resp.
v
) updated its parent in round
j
, and this adds
O(|E|)
total work to Step
1
; in each iteration
j
of Step
2
, the algorithm uses all vertex processors to check all cells corresponding to round
j
(by index range), and if the cell contains a vertex
v
then executes
v.p=v.p.p
, so the iteration takes
O(|V(E)|)
work and
O(1)
time.
Since only vertex with an adjacent edge can update its parent, this implementation is correct, and Step
2
takes
O(k)
time and
O(k\cdot|V(E)|)
work.

In Step
3
, each edge
(u,v)\in E
notifies the vertices
u
and
v
to be in
V^{\prime}
. This takes
O(1)
time and
O(|E|)
work, giving the lemma.
∎

Lemma 4.23
.

Let
k=10^{6}\log\log n
.

Reduce
(V,E,k)
can be implemented on an ARBITRARY CRCW PRAM in
O(\log\log n)
time and
O(m)+\overline{O}(n)
work w.p.
1-n^{-3}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.20
, Step
1
takes
o(\log\log n)
time and
O(m)+\overline{O}(n)
work, and reduces the number of vertices in the current graph to
n/\log\log n
w.p.
1-n^{-4}
. In the following, we shall assume that this event holds and apply a union bound at the end.

After Step
1
, the algorithm runs approximate compaction in
O(\log^{*}n)
time to rename each vertex to a unique id in
[2n/\log\log n]
in
O(n)
work w.p.
1-1/2^{n^{1/25}}\geq 1-n^{-10}
(Lemma
4.2
).

By Lemma
4.22
, Step
2
takes
O(\log\log n)
time and
O(m+k\cdot n/\log\log n)=O(m+n)
work.

Step
3
takes
O(1)
time and
O(m+n)
work.

The edge set
E^{\prime}
defined in Step
4
is the same as the edge set
E^{\prime}
defined in round
0
of
Extract
(E,k)
, i.e., let
V^{\prime}=\textsc{Filter}(E,k)
, then
E^{\prime}
is the set of edges such that at least one end of the edge is not in
V^{\prime}
.
So, we can use the same technique used in the proof of Lemma
4.15
to argue that the expected contribution to
|E^{\prime}|
from each vertex in
V(E)
is at most

(1-10^{-4})^{-j}+1=2,

where
j=0
.
Therefore,
\E[|E^{\prime}|]=O(n/\log\log n)
.

By Lemma
4.3
, the
Matching
(E^{\prime})
and
Alter
(E^{\prime})
in each round
i
in Step
5
takes
O(1)
time and
O(|E^{\prime}|)
work.
By the first paragraph,
|V|\leq n/\log\log n
, and the
v.p=v.p.p
takes
O(1)
time.
Combining with the previous paragraph, Step
5
takes
O(k)
time and
\overline{O}(k\cdot n/\log\log n)=\overline{O}(n)
total work.

Step
6
takes
O(1)
time and
O(m+n)
work. The lemma follows from a union bound.
∎

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
k=10^{6}\log\log n
.

Reduce
(V,E,k)
reduces the number of vertices in the current graph to
n/(\log n)^{1000}
w.p.
1-n^{-7}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.21
, the algorithm is a correct contraction algorithm.
Apply Definition
4.11
and use the same technique in the proof of Lemma
4.14
, we have that
V^{\prime}
defined in Step
2
has cardinality at most

0.999^{k+i}n=0.999^{10^{6}\log\log n}\leq\frac{n}{(\log n)^{1400}}

w.p.
1-n^{-8}
, where
i=0
as we only call
Filter
(E,k)
once.

By the proof of Lemma
4.21
and Step
4
, all active roots not in
V^{\prime}
must have an adjacent edge in
E^{\prime}
, so now we bound the number of active roots in
V(E^{\prime})
.
(Recall that an active root is a vertex that is a root with adjacent edges to another root in the current graph.)
A vertex
v
is called
alive
if
v
is a root in the labeled digraph and has an adjacent edge in
E^{\prime}
.
(Note that an active root might not be alive since
E^{\prime}
does not contain all the edges in the current graph.)
By an induction on
i\in[0,k]
in Step
5
and applying Lemma
4.4
, the number of alive vertices at the beginning of round
i
is at most
0.999^{i}n
w.p.
1-i\cdot n^{-9}
. So, the number of alive vertices before Step
6
is at most
0.999^{10^{6}\log\log n}\leq n/(\log n)^{1400}
w.p.
1-n^{-8}
.

Consider a non-alive vertex
v
in the subgraph
G^{\prime}
induced on
E^{\prime}
before Step
6
.
If the component of
v
in
G^{\prime}
does not contain a vertex in
V^{\prime}
, then the computation for this component is done as
G^{\prime}
contains all the edges between vertices in this component, so
v
is not active since there is no edge on
v
in the current graph by the fact that
v
is the root of a flat tree and the execution of
Alter
(E^{\prime})
.
Otherwise, if the component of
v
in
G^{\prime}
does contain a vertex in
V^{\prime}
, then the
Reverse
(V^{\prime},E)
in Step
6
makes
v
a non-root and thus not an active root at the end of
Reduce
(V,E,k)
(and this root is counted through the first paragraph of this proof).

As a result, the number of vertices in the current graph at the end of
Reduce
(V,E,k)
is at most
2n/(\log n)^{1400}\leq n/(\log n)^{1000}
w.p.
1-n^{-7}
.
∎

Lemma 4.25
.

Let
k=10^{6}\log\log n
.

Reduce
(V,E,k)
runs on an ARBITRARY CRCW PRAM in
O(m)+\overline{O}(n)
work and
O(\log\log n)
time, and reduces the number of vertices in the current graph to
n/(\log n)^{1000}
w.p.
1-n^{-2}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.23
and Lemma
4.24
.
∎

We did not optimize the constants in this section.
The setup of
k
is a consequence of §
7
.

5
Stage 2: Increasing the Minimum Degree to
\poly(\log n)

After Stage
1
, by Lemma
4.25
we assume the number of vertices is
n/b^{10}
where
b=(\log n)^{100}
is a parameter (we will gradually increase the value of
b
in §
7
). Let the current graph be
G=(V,E)
and we have
|V|\leq n/b^{10}
and
|E|\leq m
.
In this section, we give a linear-work algorithm that increases
\deg(G)
, the minimum degree over all vertices in
V
, to at least
\text{poly}(b)
.

Our algorithm, called
Increase
(V,E,b)
, first builds a subgraph
H
(called
skeleton graph
) on the same vertex set
V
as the input graph but with fewer edges (
\textsc{Build}(V,E,b)
), then it calls
Densify
(H,b)
to contract the skeleton graph such that some vertices obtains
\text{poly}(b)
children, then in Step
3
each vertex either
(i) becomes a non-root, or
(ii) becomes a root with
\text{poly}(b)
children.
At the end of the algorithm, any vertex in the current graph has degree at least
\text{poly}(b)
.

Increase
(V,E,b)
:

1.

Skeleton graph
H=(V,E^{\prime})=\textsc{Build}(V,E,b)
.

2.

(V,E_{\text{close}})=\textsc{Densify}(H,b)
.

3.

Use
E_{\text{close}}
and the labeled digraph updated in Step
2
to increase the degree of each root (vertex in the current graph) to be at least
\text{poly}(b)
– see §
5.3
for a detailed description.

In this section, we state the algorithm’s guarantees in terms of
good
rather than
high
probability, where good probability means with probability
1-1/\poly\log n
. Later in §
8
, we will be able to boost the probability guarantees to high probability.

5.1
Building the Skeleton Graph

Build
(V,E,b)
:

1.

For each vertex
v\in V
: assign a block of
b^{9}
processors, which will be used as an indexed hash table
\mathcal{H}(v)
of size
b^{9}
.

2.

Choose a random hash function
h:[n]\to[b^{9}]
. For each edge
(u,v)\in E
: write
u
into the
h(u)
-th cell of
\mathcal{H}(v)
.

3.

For each vertex
v\in V
: if the number of vertices in
\mathcal{H}(v)
is more than
b^{8}
then mark
v
as
high
, else mark
v
as
low
.

4.

Let
E^{\prime}
be an empty edge set. For each edge
(u,v)\in E
: if
u
or
v
is low then add
(u,v)
to
E^{\prime}
, else add
(u,v)
to
E^{\prime}
w.p.
b^{-1}
.

5.

Remove parallel edges and loops from
E^{\prime}
.

6.

Return graph
H=(V,E^{\prime})
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Build
(V,E,b)
can be implemented on an ARBITRARY CRCW PRAM in
O(\log b)
time and
O(m+n)
work w.p.
1-n^{-8}
.

Proof.

Recall that
|V|\leq n/b^{10}
and
b=(\log n)^{100}
.
In Step
1
, using approximate compaction (Lemma
4.2
) we compact the vertices in
V
to an index array then use their indices to assign a block of indexed
b^{4}
processors, which takes
O(n)
work and
O(\log^{*}n)
time w.p.
1-n^{-9}
.
In Step
2
, each vertex
u
computes
h(u)
by choosing an index from
[b^{9}]
uniformly at random and independently then stores
h(u)
in the private memory of
u
. Next, each edge
(u,v)
reads the value of
h(u)
from
u
then writes into
H(v)
. The total work is
O(m+n)
and the running time is
O(1)
.
In Step
3
, for each vertex
v
, use all the
b^{9}
processors in its block to count the number of cells occupied by any vertex in
\mathcal{H}(v)
(by a simple binary tree structure) in
O(\log(b^{9}))=O(\log b)
time, whose work is at most
|V|\cdot b^{9}\cdot O(\log b)\leq O(n)
.
In Step
4
, each edge decides whether itself is in
E^{\prime}
in
O(1)
time, which takes
O(m)
total work.

In Step
5
, loops can be removed in
O(m)
work and
O(1)
time.
To remove parallel edges, we use PRAM perfect hashing on (the indices of) all edges of
E^{\prime}
in
O(m)
work and
O(\log^{*}n)
time w.p.
1-n^{-9}

[
GMV91
]
.
7
7

7

Given a set
S\subseteq[m]
, the
perfect hashing
problem is to find an one-to-one function
h:S\to[O(|S|)]
such that
h
can be represented in
O(|S|)
space and
h(x)
can be evaluated in
O(1)
time for any
x\in S
. We use perfect hashing to distinguish from other hashings used in this paper which can cause collisions.

The lemma follows from a union bound.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
G=(V,E)
.
W.p.
1-n^{-9}
, for any vertex
v\in V
that is marked as high in
Build
(V,E,b)
,
|N_{G}(v)|\geq b^{8}
;
and for any vertex
v\in V
that is marked as low in
Build
(V,E,b)
,
|N_{G}(v)|\leq b^{9}
.

Proof.

For any high vertex
v
, it must have at least
b^{8}
neighbors by Step
3
, giving the first part of the lemma.

A vertex
v
is low if at most
b^{8}
cells in
\mathcal{H}(v)
are occupied (Step
3
).
If
|N_{G}(v)|\geq b^{9}
, then
v
is low w.p. at most

\binom{b^{9}}{b^{8}}\cdot\left(\frac{b^{8}}{b^{9}}\right)^{b^{9}}\leq b^{9b^{8}}\cdot b^{-b^{9}}\leq b^{-b^{8}}\leq n^{-10},

where we used
b=(\log n)^{100}
. So, w.p.
1-n^{-9}
all such vertices are marked as high by a union bound and we shall condition on this happening.
Since any vertex
v
with
|N_{G}(v)|\geq b^{9}
is marked as high, we have that any low vertex
v
must have
|N_{G}(v)|\leq b^{9}
. This gives the second part of the lemma.
∎

We are ready to give two key properties of the subgraph
H
returned by
Build
(V,E,b)
.

Definition 5.3
.

Given any graph
G=(V,E)
and a vertex
v\in V
, let
\mathcal{C}_{G}(v)
be the component in
G
that contains
v
, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}_{G}(v)
is a vertex-induced subgraph of
G
on all vertices with paths to
v
in
G
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Given any graph
G=(V,E)
, let
H=\textsc{Build}(V,E,b)
. W.p.
1-n^{-8}
, for any vertex
v\in V
, either
V(\mathcal{C}_{G}(v))=V(\mathcal{C}_{H}(v))
or
|V(\mathcal{C}_{H}(v))|\geq b^{6}
.

Proof.

For any vertex
v\in V
, consider
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
(and thus
E^{\prime}
and
H
) before Step
5
.

For the purpose of analysis, perform breath-first searches on
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
and
\mathcal{C}_{G}(v)
simultaneously, starting at
v
.
At any time of the breath-first searches, let
u
be the vertex that is currently exploring. If
u
is low, then all of the adjacent edges of
u
are added into
E^{\prime}
by Step
4
.
So, by an induction (on vertices in any breath-first search order), these two breath-first searches perform the same computations (the order of examined edges and explored vertices are identical) before exploring a high vertex.
Therefore, if there is no high vertex in
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
, all vertices in
\mathcal{C}_{G}(v)
and their adjacent edges in
G
are in
H
, giving
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)=\mathcal{C}_{G}(v)
.

On the other hand, if there is a high vertex
u
in
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
, then in Step
5
, each neighbor
w
of
u
in
G
is added into
H
w.p. at least
b^{-1}
(if
w
is low then it is added w.p.
1
).
By Lemma
5.2
, w.p.
1-n^{-9}
there are at least
b^{8}
such
w
. Conditioned on this and apply a Chernoff bound, w.p.
1-n^{-10}
, at least
b^{6}
neighbors
w^{\prime}
of
u
are added into
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
(by adding edge
(u,w^{\prime})
into
E^{\prime}
).
By a union bound, w.p.
1-n^{-8}
, we have
|V(\mathcal{C}_{H}(v))|\geq b^{6}
.

Observe that Step
5
does not change
V(\mathcal{C}_{H}(v))
(but only changes
E(\mathcal{C}_{H}(v))
). As a result, w.p.
1-n^{-8}
, it must be either
V(\mathcal{C}_{G}(v))=V(\mathcal{C}_{H}(v))
(from the second paragraph of this proof) or
|V(\mathcal{C}_{H}(v))|\geq b^{6}
.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Given any graph
G=(V,E)
, let
H=\textsc{Build}(V,E,b)
, then
|E(H)|\leq(m+n)/(\log n)^{5}
w.p.
1-n^{-8}
.

Proof.

Each edge in
E^{\prime}
is either adjacent to a low vertex or between two high vertices.

By Lemma
5.2
, w.p.
1-n^{-9}
, any low vertex
v
has at most
b^{9}
neighbors in
G
, which means
v
has at most
b^{9}
neighbors in
H
as the algorithm only deletes edges from
E
to obtain
E^{\prime}
(Step
4
). By Step
5
, the number of edges adjacent on
v
is exactly the number of neighbors in
H
. Combining, the number of edges in
H
adjacent to low vertices is at most
|V|\cdot b^{9}\leq n/b
w.p.
1-n^{-9}
.

Assume the number of edges between high vertices is at least
n/(\log n)^{6}
in
G
(otherwise the proof is done).
After Step
4
, by a Chernoff bound, the number of edges between high vertices in
E^{\prime}
is at most
(|E^{\prime}|\log n)/b\leq(m\log n)/b
w.p.
1-n^{-9}
. Step
5
only removes edges from
E^{\prime}
.

By a union bound, w.p.
1-n^{-8}
, the number of edges in
E^{\prime}
is at most
n/b+(m\log n)/b
, which is at most
(m+n)/(\log n)^{5}
by
b=(\log n)^{100}
.
∎

5.2
Running the Densify Algorithm on the Skeleton Graph

In this section, we introduce the algorithm
Densify
, which calls the subroutine
Expand-Maxlink
for certain number of rounds.

5.2.1
Algorithmic Framework of
Densify

The algorithm
Expand-Maxlink
(presented later) is from
[
LTZ20
]
with minor changes, which uses the following two subroutines.

Shortcut
(V)
: for each vertex
v\in V
:
v.p=v.p.p
.

The
Shortcut
is a standard operation in PRAM algorithms for connected components (e.g., see
[
LT19
,
LT22
]
).

Maxlink
(V)
: repeat {for each vertex
v\in V
: let
u\coloneqq\argmax_{w\in N^{*}(v).p}\ell(w)
, if
\ell(u)>\ell(v)
then
v.p=u
} for
2
iterations.

For any vertex
v
, let
N^{*}(v)\coloneqq N(v)\cup\{v\}
, i.e.,
N^{*}(v)
is the set of neighbors of
v
plus itself.
For any vertex set
S
, define
N^{*}(S)\coloneqq\bigcup_{w\in S}N^{*}(w)
,
and define
S.p\coloneqq\{w.p\mid w\in S\}
.

The
level

\ell(v)
of a vertex
v
is a non-negative integer that monotonically increases during the algorithm, starting from
1
.
Given
\beta\geq 0
, we say a vertex
v
has
budget

\beta(v)\coloneqq\beta
if the maximum-size block owned by
v
has size
\beta
(a vertex can own more than
1
blocks, but only the block that is most recently assigned to
v
has the maximum size; the non-maximum-size blocks contain the added edges and thus cannot be ignored, but when the algorithm does hashing it only uses the maximum-size block).
A root with level
\ell
is assigned to a block of size

\beta_{\ell}\coloneqq\beta_{1}^{1.01^{\ell-1}}\text{~with~}\beta_{1}=(\log n)^{80}

(2)

in Step
9
of
Expand-Maxlink
.
Each block of size
\beta
is partitioned into
\sqrt{\beta}
(indexed) tables, each with size
\sqrt{\beta}
.
We assume each vertex in the current graph owns a block of size
\beta_{1}
before any application of
Expand-Maxlink
, which can be guaranteed by approximate compaction in
O(\log^{*}n)
time w.h.p.
Therefore, the hash table of a vertex
v
(denoted by
\mathcal{H}(v)
and, without loss of generality, let it be the first indexed table in the block of
v
) has size
(\log n)^{40}
initially.

The hashing in Step
4
is computed by letting the processor corresponding to edge
(v,w)
choose a random index from
[0,|\mathcal{H}(v)|-1]
.
(Note that the edge
(v,w)\in E
in the current graph can be an (altered) edge from the input graph, or be an edge added by
Expand-Maxlink
in the form of
(v,w)
where
w
is an item in
\mathcal{H}(v)
, so
Alter
(E)
also applies to those added edges.)
Since each such edge owns a processor and
|\mathcal{H}(v)|
can be computed in
O(1)
time by the budget of
v
, the hashing can be done in
O(1)
time.
Step
6
follows the same manner.

Expand-Maxlink
(H)
:

1.

Let
V=V(H)
and
E=E(H)
.

2.

Maxlink
(V)
;
Alter
(E)
.

3.

For each root
v\in V
: increase
\ell(v)
by
1
w.p.
\beta(v)^{-0.06}
.

4.

For each root
v\in V
: for each root
w\in N^{*}(v)
: if
\beta(w)=\beta(v)
then hash
w
into
\mathcal{H}(v)
.

5.

For each root
v\in V
: if there is a collision in
\mathcal{H}(v)
then mark
v
as dormant.
For each vertex
v\in V
: if there is a dormant vertex in
\mathcal{H}(v)
then mark
v
as dormant.

6.

For each root
v\in V
: for each
w\in\mathcal{H}(v)
: for each
u\in\mathcal{H}(w)
: hash
u
into
\mathcal{H}(v)
. For each root
v\in V
: if there is a collision in
\mathcal{H}(v)
then mark
v
as dormant.

7.

Maxlink
(V)
;
Shortcut
(V)
;
Alter
(E(V))
.

8.

For each root
v\in V
: if
v
is dormant and did not increase its level in Step
3
then increase
\ell(v)
by
1
.

9.

For each root
v\in V
: assign a block of size
\beta_{\ell(v)}
to
v
.

10.

Return
H
as the current graph with all added edges (in the form of items in all hash tables).

Densify
(H,b)
:

1.

For
round

i
from
0
to
20\log b
:
H=\textsc{Expand-Maxlink}(H)
.

2.

Let
V
be the set of roots in
H
.

3.

Repeat for
2\log\log\log n
times:
Shortcut
(V(H))
.
Alter
(E(H))
.

4.

Let
E_{\text{close}}
be the set of edges in the current graph
H
, including all (altered) edges from the input graph and all (altered) edges in all hash tables of all vertices.

5.

Call the algorithm in Theorem
2
on graph
(V(E_{\text{close}}),E_{\text{close}})
to update the labeled digraph.

6.

Alter
(E_{\text{close}})
.

7.

Return
(V,E_{\text{close}})
.

The algorithm
Densify
(H,b)
calls
\textsc{Expand-Maxlink}(H)
for
O(\log b)
times and gradually changes the skeleton graph
H
by adding edges (Step
10
in
\textsc{Expand-Maxlink}(H)
), where
b=(\log n)^{100}
is fixed in this section.
We will show that the total work of
Densify
(H,b)
is
O((m+n)/\poly(\log n))
w.h.p., and the final skeleton graph
H
(at the end of round
100\log b
) is still sparse (
O((m+n)/\poly(\log n))
edges) w.h.p.
8
8

8

The algorithm in Theorem
2
proceeds in rounds, where each round updates the labeled digraph corresponding to
G
and takes
O(1)
time.

5.2.2
Properties of
Densify

In this section, we prove the key properties of the algorithm
Densify
that calls
Expand-Maxlink
repeatedly.
Some properties of
Expand-Maxlink
are already presented in
[
LTZ20
]
.

Lemma 5.6
.

W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
, Step
1
of
Densify
(H,b)
uses
O((m+n)/(\log n)^{4})
processors.

Proof.

In
[
LTZ20
]
, it is proved that w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{10}
, the first
O(\log n)
rounds of
\textsc{Expand-Maxlink}(G^{\prime})
uses
O(|E(G^{\prime})|+|V(G^{\prime})|)
processors
9
9

9

[
LTZ20
]
assumes
|E(G^{\prime})|\geq 2|V(G^{\prime})|
for simplicity in presenting bounds.
(see their Lemma 5.3).
Since
b=(\log n)^{100}
in this section,
Densify
(H,b)
calls
O(\log\log n)
rounds of
H=\textsc{Expand-Maxlink}(H)
and the number of processors used is
O(|E(H)|+|V(H)|)
.
By the assumption at the beginning of §
5
,
|V(H)|\leq n/b^{10}
.
By Lemma
5.5
,
|E(H)|\leq(m+n)/(\log n)^{5}
w.p.
1-n^{-8}
.
The lemma follows by
b=(\log n)^{100}
.
∎

Later in §
7
, we will gradually increase the value of
b
, but it will be at most
n
, so Lemma
5.6
can still be applied.

Lemma 5.7
.

W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
, during the execution of
Densify
(H,b)
, the level
\ell(v)
of any vertex
v\in V(H)
is at most
100\log\log n
.

Proof.

Assume
Densify
(H,b)
uses
O((m+n)/(\log n)^{4})
processors.
By examining
Maxlink
(V)
– the only place that updates parents in
\textsc{Expand-Maxlink}(H)
in
Densify
(H,b)
– we have that
\ell(v)\leq\ell(v.p)
for any vertex
v
, which holds at any time since the level increase only happens on roots (Step
3
and Step
8
) whose parents are themselves.
Assume for contradiction that there exists a vertex
v\in V(H)
such that
\ell(v)>100\log\log n
.
The root
u
of the tree (in the labeled digraph) containing
v
must have
\ell(u)>100\log\log n
.
In Step
9
of
\textsc{Expand-Maxlink}(H)
, root
u
is assigned a block of size
\beta_{\ell(u)}
, which, by Equation (
2
), implies that the number of processors in the block owned by
u
is

\beta(u)=\beta_{\ell(u)}\geq\beta_{1}^{1.01^{100\log\log n}}\geq((\log n)^{80})^{\log n}\geq n^{80}\geq m+n,

contradicting with the assumption we made at the beginning of this proof.
The lemma then follows from Lemma
5.6
.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{6}
, any round of
Densify
(H,b)
can be implemented on an ARBITRARY CRCW PRAM in
O(1)
time.

Proof.

By an induction on round
i
from
0
to
20\log b
, we assume each edge and each vertex in the current graph
H
has a corresponding processor, which holds at the beginning of round
0
.
Consider the
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
in round
i
of
Densify
(H,b)
.

Firstly, we show that the
Maxlink
(V)
in Step
2
and Step
7
takes
O(1)
time.
It suffices to compute
u\coloneqq\argmax_{w\in N^{*}(v).p}\ell(w)
for each
v\in V
in
O(1)
time.
We could not compute the maximum level over the neighbors parents set directly in constant time.
Observe from Lemma
5.7
that w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
, all levels are in
[100\log\log n]
.
Using the second indexed table in the block of
v
(whose size is at least
\sqrt{\beta_{1}}=(\log n)^{40}
), the processor corresponding to edge
(w^{\prime},v)
reads
w=w^{\prime}.p
from
w^{\prime}
then reads
\ell(w)
from
w
, for each
w^{\prime}\in N^{*}(v)
. Next, this processor writes
w
into the
\ell(w)
-th cell of the second indexed table in the block of
v
. Since such
w
’s are within the first
100\log\log n
cells, we can use
\log n
processors from the third indexed table of
v
to determine the
w
with maximum level
\ell(w)
by assigning each pair from
[100\log\log n]\times[100\log\log n]
a corresponding processor, giving
u\coloneqq\argmax_{w\in N^{*}(v).p}\ell(w)
.

Secondly, we show that the hashings in Step
4
and Step
6
take
O(1)
time.
Step
4
is straightforward since each
w\in N^{*}(v)
has a corresponding processor, and the hashing is computed in
O(1)
time as explained before the pseudocode.
To implement Step
6
, each vertex
u\in\mathcal{H}(w)
such that
w\in\mathcal{H}(v)
needs to own a corresponding processor. Note that by Step
4
, it must be
\beta(w)=\beta(v)
, so we have that for each
w\in\mathcal{H}(v)
, the table size of
w
is exactly
\sqrt{\beta(w)}=\sqrt{\beta(v)}
. Since
v
owns
\beta(v)
processors in its block, we can assign a processor to each cell in the table of such
w
for each
w\in\mathcal{H}(v)
(the table size of
v
is
\sqrt{\beta(v)}
) allocated by their indices.
Therefore, in
O(1)
time we use the processor corresponding to
u
to write
u
into
\mathcal{H}(v)
. The added edge
(u,v)
owns a corresponding processor – the same processor in
\mathcal{H}(v)
.

Thirdly, we show that Step
9
can be implemented in
O(1)
time.
By Lemma
5.6
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
, the total number of processors used is at most
(m+n)/(\log n)^{3}
.
The pool of
(m+n)/(\log n)^{3}
processors is partitioned into
(\log n)^{2}
zones such that the processor allocation in round
i\in[20\log b]
for vertices with level
\ell
uses the zone indexed by
(i,\ell)
, where
\ell\in[100\log\log n]
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
by Lemma
5.7
.
Since there are
(m+n)/(\log n)^{3}
processors in total and all the vertex ids are in
[n/b^{10}]
as assumed at the beginning of §
5
, we can use
O(n/b^{10}\cdot\log n)
processors (such that there are
\Theta(\log n)
processors per vertex) for each different level and apply a tighten version of Lemma
4.2
(see
[
LTZ20
]
for details) to index each root in
O(1)
time with probability
1-n^{-9}
such that the indices of vertices with the same level are distinct, then assign each of them a distinct block in the corresponding zone.
11
11

11

The number of processor used for this part is actually already counted in Lemma
5.6
.

Therefore, Step
9
takes
O(1)
time w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
by a union bound over all
100\log\log n
levels.

All other computations in the
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
in round
i
of
Densify
(H,b)
can be done in
O(1)
time, and each edge and each vertex has a corresponding processor at the end of round
i
. This completes the induction on
i
and gives the lemma by a union bound over all rounds.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, at the end of
Densify
(H,b)
, for any vertex
v\in V(H)
,
v
is either a root or a child of a root,
12
12

12

We did not state that all trees in the labeled digraph are flat because the vertex set
V(H)
does not contain all the vertices in the original input graph:
H
is the current graph after Stage
1
that induced on roots. The non-roots created in Stage
1
can be as many as
\Theta(n)
so that we cannot run
Shortcut
on all of them. Instead, we will prove that at the end of Stage
3
, all such non-roots are children or grandchildren of roots so that one application of
Shortcut
flattens all trees and thus gives a correct connected components algorithm, which adds
O(n)
work in total.

and both ends of any edge in
E_{\text{close}}
are roots.

Proof.

The only place that updates parents is in
Maxlink
(V)
, which gives
\ell(v)<\ell(v.p)
for any non-root
v
.
Note that this relation holds at any time of the algorithm, because the level increase only happens on roots (Step
3
and Step
8
within
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
).
As a result, the distance from any vertex
v\in V(H)
to the root of the tree containing
v
is at most the maximum level, which is at most
100\log\log n
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
by Lemma
5.7
.
(The vertices in not in
V(H)
must be leaves whose parents are in
V(H)
since Stage
1
is a contraction algorithm, also see footnote
12
.)
One iteration of
Shortcut
(V(H))
in Step
3
of
Densify
(H,b)
reduces the distance
\delta
from a vertex
v
to the root of the tree containing
v
to at most
2\delta/3
if
\delta\geq 2
(the worst case is reducing the distance from
3
to
2
, see
[
LT19
]
for details).
Therefore, after
2\log\log n\geq\log_{3/2}(100\log\log n)
iterations of
Shortcut
(V(H))
, the distance from any vertex
v\in V(H)
to the root of the tree containing
v
is at most
1
.
The following
Alter
(E(H))
in Step
3
makes all edges adjacent to roots.
Therefore, the contraction algorithm in Theorem
2
operates on all roots with adjacent edges (in
E_{\text{close}}
) and maintains the invariant that each vertex in
V(H)
is either a root or a child of a root at the end of Step
5
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
, giving the first part of the lemma.
The
Alter
(E_{\text{close}})
in Step
6
moves all edges in
E_{\text{close}}
adjacent to roots.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{3}
,
Densify
(H,b)
can be implemented on an ARBITRARY CRCW PRAM in
O(\log b)
time and
(m+n)/(\log n)^{3}
work.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.6
and Lemma
5.8
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{5}
, Step
1
of
Densify
(H,b)
takes
O(\log b)
time and
O(m+n)/(\log n)^{4}
work.
Consider Step
3
. By the assumption at the beginning of §
5
,
|V(H)|\leq n/b^{10}\leq n/(\log n)^{5}
by
b=(\log n)^{100}
.
So the
2\log\log n
iterations of
Shortcut
(V(H))
takes at most
n/(\log n)^{4}
work and
\log b
time.
By Lemma
5.5
,
|E(H_{0})|\leq(m+n)/(\log n)^{5}
w.p.
1-n^{-8}
, where
H_{0}
is the graph
H
passed to
Expand-Maxlink
in round
0
.
The number of edges added into
H
over all rounds in Step
1
is upper bounded by the total work performed over all rounds of
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
, which is
O(m+n)/(\log n)^{4}
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{5}
by the first sentence of this proof.
By a union bound, the work in the first
4
steps of
Densify
(H,b)
is at most
O(m+n)/(\log n)^{4}
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{4}
.
Note that
E_{\text{close}}
is exactly the
E(H)
at the end of Step
4
, so w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
, Step
5
takes
O(m+n)/(\log n)^{4}\cdot 100\log\log n\leq(m+n)/(\log n)^{3.5}
work and
100\log\log n\leq\log b
time.
Step
6
takes
O(1)
time and
O(E_{\text{close}})\leq O(m+n)/(\log n)^{4}
work.
Summing up and by a union bound, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{3}
, all steps take
O(\log b)
time and
(m+n)/(\log n)^{3}
work in total.
∎

We use one crucial property of
Expand-Maxlink
from
[
LTZ20
]
(stated as Lemma 5.24 in their full version), which will be used later.

Lemma 5.11

(
[
LTZ20
]
)
.

For any root
v\in V(H)
and any vertex
u\in N^{*}(N^{*}(v))
at the beginning of any round
i
within
Densify
(H,b)
,
let
u^{\prime}
be the parent of
u
after Step
2
of the
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
in round
i
.
If
v
does not increase level and is a root during round
i
, then
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}(v)
after Step
6
of the
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
within round
i
.

5.2.3
Path Construction for
Densify

Fix any shortest path
P
in graph
H
.
The length of
P
is not necessarily
d_{G}
– the diameter of the original input graph
G
, as shown in §
B
.

Consider the changes on
P
over rounds of
Densify
(H,b)
.
The
Alter
(E)
(Step
2
and Step
7
) in each round replaces an edge
(v,w)
on
P
by
(v.p,w.p)
.
The path
P
also changes when adding edges to the current graph:
for any vertices
v
and
w
on path
P
, if the current graph contains edge
(v,w)
, then all vertices between
v
and
w
(exclusively) can be removed from
P
, which still results in a valid path in the current graph from the first to the last vertex of
P
, reducing the length of
P
.
We use
|P|
as the number of vertices on
P
, which is
1
plus the length of
P
.
For any integer
j\in[0,|P|-1]
, let
P(j)
be the
j
-th vertex on
P
.

Formally, we have the following inductive construction for path changes:

PathConstruction
(H,P)
:

Let all vertices on
P
be
active
at the beginning of round
0
of
Densify
(H,b)
.

For any round
i\in[0,9\log b)
, given path
P_{i}
with at least
4
active vertices at the beginning of round
i
,
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
constructs
P_{i+1}
by the following
6
phases:

1.

The
Alter
(E)
in Step
2
replaces each vertex
v
on
P_{i}
by
v^{\prime}\coloneqq v.p
to get path
P_{i,1}
. Set
v^{\prime}
as active or passive according to
v
. For any
v^{\prime}
on
P_{i,1}
, let
\underline{v^{\prime}}
be on
P_{i}
such that
\underline{v^{\prime}}.p=v^{\prime}
.

2.

After Step
6
, set
j
as
1
, and repeat the following until
j\geq|P_{i,1}|-1
or
P_{i,1}(j+1)
is
passive
(not active):

•

Let
v^{\prime}\coloneqq P_{i,1}(j)
, if
\underline{v^{\prime}}
is a root and does not increase level during round
i
then:

(a)

If the current graph contains edge
(v^{\prime},P_{i,1}(j+2))
then mark
P_{i,1}(j+1)
as
skipped
and set
j\coloneqq j+2
.

(b)

Else set
j\coloneqq j+1
.

3.

Let
k
be the maximum integer such that
P_{i,1}(k)
is active and
k\leq j
. Mark
P_{i,1}(k)
as passive.

4.

Remove all skipped and passive vertices from
P_{i,1}
to get path
P_{i,4}
.

5.

Concatenate
P_{i,4}
with all passive vertices after
P_{i,1}(|P_{i,4}|)
(exclusively) on
P_{i,1}
to get path
P_{i,5}
.

6.

The
Alter
(E)
in Step
7
replaces each vertex
v
on
P_{i,5}
by
v.p
to get path
P_{i+1}
. Set
v.p
as active or passive according to
v
.

For any vertex
v
on
P_{i}
that is replaced by
v^{\prime}
in Phase
1
, if
v^{\prime}
is not removed in Phase
4
, then let
\overline{v}
be the vertex replacing
v^{\prime}
in Phase
6
, and call
\overline{v}
the
corresponding vertex
of
v
in round
i+1
.

13
13

13

The definitions of
\underline{v^{\prime}}
and
\overline{v}
are for our later potential argument. In Phase
1
, if there are two vertices
P_{i}(x)
and
P_{i}(y)
with the same parent
v^{\prime}
where
x\neq y
, then
\underline{P_{i,1}(x)}=P_{i}(x)
and
\underline{P_{i,1}(y)}=P_{i}(y)
, which resolves ambiguity by their indices. Phase
6
follows the same manner. We could have defined potentials on indices on the path rather than on the vertices, but this involves too many parameters to parse.

To obtain bounds on the length of
P
over rounds, we need the following potential function.

Definition 5.12
.

For any (active) vertex
v
on path
P_{0}\coloneqq P
, define its
potential

\Phi_{0}(v)\coloneqq 1
.
For any integer
i\in[0,9\log b)
, given path
P_{i}
with at least
4
active vertices at the beginning of round
i
of
Densify
(H,b)
, define the
potential
of each vertex on
P_{i+1}
based on
PathConstruction
(H,P)
by the following steps:

1.

For each vertex
v
on
P_{i}
that is replaced by
v.p
in Phase
1
,
\Phi_{i,1}(v.p)\coloneqq\Phi_{i}(v)
.

2.

After Phase
3
, for each active vertex
v
on
P_{i,1}
, if the successor
w
of
v
is skipped or passive then
\Phi_{i,3}(v)\coloneqq\Phi_{i,1}(v)+\Phi_{i,1}(w)
and
\Phi_{i,3}(w)\coloneqq 0
.

3.

For each vertex
v
on
P_{i,1}
where
\Phi_{i,3}(v)
is not updated in Step
2
,
\Phi_{i,3}(v)\coloneqq\Phi_{i,1}(v)
.

4.

For each vertex
v
on
P_{i,5}
that is replaced by
v.p
in Phase
6
,
\Phi_{i+1}(v.p)\coloneqq\Phi_{i,3}(v)
.

The following lemma is immediate:

Lemma 5.13
.

For any integer
i\in[0,9\log b)
, let path
P_{i}
with at least
4
active vertices be defined in
PathConstruction
(H,P)
, then

\sum_{v\text{~is~on~}P_{i}}\Phi_{i}(v)\geq\sum_{v\text{~is~on~}P_{i+1}}\Phi_{i+1}(v)
.

Proof Sketch.

In Definition
5.12
,
(i) the mapping in Step
1
is one-to-one (see footnote
13
);
(ii) in Step
2
, the skipped or passive vertex
w
passes its potential to
v
;
(iii) in Step
3
, only a vertex not updates its potential in Step
2
inherits the potential;
(iv) the mapping in Step
4
is one-to-one.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

For any integer
i\in[0,9\log b)
, let path
P_{i}
with at least
4
active vertices be defined in
PathConstruction
(H,P)
.
For any active vertex
v
on
P_{i}
,
\Phi_{i}(v)\geq 2^{i-\ell(v)}
.

Proof.

The proof is by an induction on
i
.
The base case holds because for any vertex
v
on
P_{0}=P
,
\Phi_{0}(r)=1\geq 2^{0-1}
.
Now we prove the inductive step from
i
to
i+1
given that the corresponding vertex
\overline{v}
of
v\in P_{i}
is on
P_{i+1}
and active.

Suppose
v
is a non-root at the end of round
i
.
If
v
is a non-root before the
Alter
(E(V))
in Step
7
within
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
within round
i
of
Densify
(H,b)
, then
\ell(v.p)>\ell(v)
by examining
Maxlink
(V)
, and
\ell(\overline{v})\geq\ell(v.p)>\ell(v)
(Phase
6
in
PathConstruction
(H,P)
).
So, by the induction hypothesis,
\Phi_{i+1}(\overline{v})\geq\Phi_{i}(v)\geq 2^{i-\ell(v)}\geq 2^{i+1-\ell(\overline{v})}
.

Suppose
v
increases its level in round
i
.
Let
\ell
be the level of
v
at the beginning of round
i
.
If the increase happens in Step
3
, then
v
is a root after Step
2
.
Whether
v
changes its parent in Step
7
or not, the level of
\overline{v}=v.p
is at least
\ell+1
.
Else, if the increase happens in Step
8
, then
v
is a root after Step
7
.
So
\overline{v}=v
and its level is at least
\ell+1
at the end of round
i
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\Phi_{i+1}(\overline{v})\geq\Phi_{i}(v)\geq 2^{i-\ell}\geq 2^{i+1-\ell(\overline{v})}
.

Since a non-root never becomes a root,
it remains to assume that
v
is a root and does not increase level during round
i
.
Since we assumed that the corresponding vertex
\overline{v}
of
v\in P_{i}
is on
P_{i+1}
and active, the vertex
v^{\prime}
that replaces
v
on
P_{i}
in Phase
1
of
PathConstruction
(H,P)
(i.e.,
\underline{v^{\prime}}.p=v^{\prime}
) is exactly
v
, and is not removed in Phase
4
and remains to be active before Phase
6
. Moreover, by Step
1
of Definition
5.12
,
\Phi_{i,1}(v^{\prime})=\Phi_{i,1}(v)=\Phi_{i}(v)
.

1.

Suppose the index
j
of
v
on
P_{i,1}
satisfies that (i)
j\geq|P_{i,1}|-1
, or (ii)
j<|P_{i,1}|-1
and
P_{i,1}(j+1)
is passive.
We prove that
j
is the maximum integer such that
P_{i,1}(j)
is active.
Case (i) is easy since
j
is the last vertex on
P_{i,1}
.
It remains to prove case (ii).
Assume for contradiction that there exists
j^{\prime}>j
such that
P_{i,1}(j^{\prime})
is active. Then we have
j^{\prime}>j+1
since
P_{i,1}(j+1)
is passive. Since
P_{i,1}(j+1)
is passive before Phase
3
of round
i
in
PathConstruction
(H,P)
, in some round
i^{\prime}<i
the
PathConstruction
(H,P)
marks
j+1
from active to passive, which means
j+1
is maximum integer such that
P_{i^{\prime},1}(j+1)
is active, and
P_{i^{\prime},1}(j^{\prime})
must be passive. This implies that the
j^{\prime}
-th vertex on this path remains to be passive starting from round
i^{\prime}
, contradicting with the fact that
P_{i,1}(j^{\prime})
is active.
As a result,
j
must be the maximum integer such that
P_{i,1}(j)
is active, and
v=P_{i,1}(j)
is marked as passive in Phase
3
of round
i
in
PathConstruction
(H,P)
.
This contradicts with the fact that the corresponding vertex
\overline{v}
of
v
is active on
P_{i+1}
(the first paragraph of this proof).

2.

Suppose the index
j
of
v
on
P_{i,1}
satisfies that
j<|P_{i,1}|-1
and
P_{i,1}(j+1)
is active.

(a)

Suppose
P_{i,1}(j+2)
is an active vertex on
P_{i,1}
.
Observe that
P_{i,1}(j+2)
is the parent
u^{\prime}
of a vertex in
N^{*}(N^{*}(v))
(where
N^{*}
is defined at the beginning of round
i
) after Step
2
within
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
of round
i
, which must be in
\mathcal{H}(v)
after Step
6
by Lemma
5.11
.
Therefore, the current graph contains edge
(v,u^{\prime})
and
w\coloneqq P_{i,1}(j+1)
is skipped by Phase
2a
in
PathConstruction
(H,P)
,
thus

\Phi_{i,3}(v)=\Phi_{i,1}(v)+\Phi_{i,1}(w)

(3)

by Step
2
in Definition
5.12
.
Next, we prove that
\ell(w)\leq\ell(v)
at the beginning of round
i
.
Assume for contradiction that
\ell(w)>\ell(v)
.
Then,
w\in N^{*}(v)
by Step
4
and Step
6
of
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
, where
N^{*}(v)
is defined in Step
4
.
Since
v
does not increase its level during round
i
,
v
is not dormant in Step
8
.
Therefore, there is no collision in the hash tables of
v
in Step
6
and Step
5
,
and there is no collision in the hash table of
w
in Step
5
.
This means
w\in\mathcal{H}(w)=N^{*}(w)
in Step
5
, and
w\in\mathcal{H}(v)
in Step
6
.
Now we have
w\in N^{*}(v)
before Step
7
.
Consider the
Maxlink
(V)
in Step
7
.
Since
v
does not increase its level and the level of
w
cannot decrease,
\ell(w)>\ell(v)
still holds at this point.
In the first iteration of
Maxlink
(V)
, there exists a
u^{\prime}\in N^{*}(v).p
such that
\ell(u^{\prime})>\ell(v)
due to the existence of
w
and the fact that
\ell(w.p)\geq\ell(w)
.
As a result,
v
will be a non-root at the end of that iteration. Since a non-root never becomes a root, we have that
v
is a non-root after this
Maxlink
(V)
, contradicting with the fact that
v
is a root in round
i
.
Therefore, we have that
\ell(w)\leq\ell(v)
at the beginning of round
i
.
Note that vertex
\underline{w}
(the vertex that is replaced by
w
in the
Alter
(E)
in Step
2
of
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
, see Phase
1
in
PathConstruction
(H,P)
) is on
P_{i}
and
\ell(\underline{w})\leq\ell(w)\leq\ell(v)
since
w=\underline{w}.p
.
Therefore, by the induction hypothesis, we obtain

\Phi_{i}(\underline{w})\geq 2^{i-\ell(\underline{w})}\geq 2^{i-\ell(v)}.

(4)

Combining, we obtain

\displaystyle\Phi_{i+1}(v)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{i,3}(v)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{i,1}(v)+\Phi_{i,1}(w)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{i}(v)+\Phi_{i}(\underline{w})

\displaystyle\geq 2^{i-\ell(v)}+2^{i-\ell(v)}=2^{i+1-\ell(v)},

where the first line is by the fact that
v
is a root in round
i
and Step
4
in Definition
5.12
,
the second line is by Equation (
3
),
the third line is by Step
1
in Definition
5.12
,
the fourth line is by the induction hypothesis on
v
and Equation (
4
).
This finishes the inductive step for this case.

(b)

Suppose
j+2\geq|P_{i,1}|
or
P_{i,1}(j+2)
is passive. Then, using the same argument in case
1
of this proof, we get that
w\coloneqq P_{i,1}(j+1)
is the last active vertex on
P_{i,1}
, and
w
is marked as passive in Phase
3
of round
i
in
PathConstruction
(H,P)
.
Since
v
is active, we have that
\Phi_{i,3}(v)=\Phi_{i,1}(v)+\Phi_{i,1}(w)
by Step
2
in Definition
5.12
.
Since
w
is active before Phase
3
of round
i
in
PathConstruction
(H,P)
, we can use the same argument in case
2a
of this proof to obtain that
\Phi_{i+1}(v)\geq 2^{i+1-\ell(v)}
.

As a result, the lemma holds for any active vertex
\overline{v}
on
P_{i+1}
, finishing the induction and giving the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.15
.

Given graph
H
and integer
b\geq(\log n)^{100}
.
Let
r=9\log b-1
.
W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
, for any shortest path
P
in
H
such that there are at most
b^{7}
vertices on
P
, there are at most
10\log b
vertices on
P_{r}
, where
P_{r}
is defined in
PathConstruction
(H,P)
.

Proof.

W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
, suppose Lemma
5.7
holds.

Firstly, we prove that there are at most
3
active vertices on
P_{r}
.
Assume for contradiction that there are at least
4
active vertices on
P_{r}
.
By an induction on round
i\in[0,r]
, Lemma
5.13
, and the fact that each vertex on
P_{0}=P
has potential
1
and the potentials are non-negative (Definition
5.12
), we get

\sum_{v\text{~is~active~and~on~}P_{r}}\Phi_{r}(v)\leq\sum_{v\text{~is~on~}P_{r}}\Phi_{r}(v)\leq b^{7}.

(5)

Since
P_{r}
has at least
4
active vertices, by Lemma
5.14
we get for any active vertex
v
on
P_{r}
,

\Phi_{r}(v)\geq 2^{r-\ell(v)}\geq 2^{9\log b-1-100\log\log n}=2^{8\log b-1}>b^{7},

where we have used
b\geq(\log n)^{100}
and
\ell(v)\leq 100\log\log n
for all
v\in V(H)
.
This contradicts with Equation (
5
).

Secondly, we show that there are at most
9\log b
passive vertices on
P_{r}
. This is because in each of the
9\log b
rounds of
PathConstruction
(H,P)
, we mark at most
1
vertex from active to passive (Phase
3
). Since all skipped vertices are removed from the path (Phase
4
), we obtain that the number of vertices on
P_{r}
is at most
9\log b+3\leq 10\log b
.
∎

Now we reduce the length of any shortest path with length at most
b^{7}
to
10\log b
.
To reduce its length even further, we need an addition path construction.

PassivePathConstruction
(H,P)
:

Let
r=9\log b-1
.
For any integer
i\in[0,r]
, let path
P_{i}
be defined as in
PathConstruction
(H,P)
.

For any integer
i\in[r,20\log b]
,
given path
P_{i}
with at least
3
vertices at the beginning of round
i
,
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
constructs
P_{i+1}
by the following
3
phases:

1.

The
Alter
(E)
in Step
2
replaces each vertex
v
on
P_{i}
by
v^{\prime}\coloneqq v.p
to get path
P_{i,1}
. For any
v^{\prime}
on
P_{i,1}
, let
\underline{v^{\prime}}
be on
P_{i}
such that
\underline{v^{\prime}}.p=v^{\prime}
.

2.

After Step
6
, let
v^{\prime}\coloneqq P_{i,1}(0)
, i.e., the first vertex on
P_{i,1}
.
If
\underline{v^{\prime}}
is a root at the end of round
i
and does not increase level during round
i
then: if the current graph contains edge
(v^{\prime},P_{i,1}(2))
then remove
P_{i,1}(1)
. After removing all such vertices from
P_{i,1}
we obtain
P_{i,2}
.

3.

The
Alter
(E)
in Step
7
replaces each vertex
v
on
P_{i,2}
by
v.p
to get path
P_{i+1}
.

For any vertex
v
on
P_{i}
that is replaced by
v^{\prime}
in Phase
1
, if
v^{\prime}
is not removed in Phase
2
, then let
\overline{v}
be the vertex replacing
v^{\prime}
in Phase
3
, and call
\overline{v}
the
corresponding vertex
of
v
in round
i+1
.

Lemma 5.16
.

Given graph
H
and integer
b\geq(\log n)^{100}
.
Let
R=20\log b
.
W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, for any shortest path
P
in
H
such that there are at most
b^{7}
vertices on
P
, there are at most
2
vertices on
P_{R}
, where
P_{R}
is defined in
PassivePathConstruction
(H,P)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.15
, at the beginning of round
r=9\log b-1
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
,
P_{r}
has length
10\log b
. We shall condition on this happening and apply a union bound at the end of the proof.

In any round
i\geq r
, for any path
P_{i}
with
|P_{i}|\geq 3
, consider the first vertex
v^{\prime}=P_{i,1}(0)
on
P_{i,1}
(defined in
PassivePathConstruction
(H,P)
).
If
\underline{v^{\prime}}
is a non-root or increases its level during round
i
, then by the first
4
paragraphs in the proof of Lemma
5.14
, it must be
\ell(\overline{v^{\prime}})\geq\ell(\underline{v^{\prime}})+1
.
If this is not the case, by Lemma
5.11
, there is an edge between
v^{\prime}
and
P_{i,1}(2)
in the graph after Step
6
, which means the successor
P_{i,1}(1)
of
v^{\prime}
on
P_{i,1}
is removed in Phase
2
of
PassivePathConstruction
(H,P)
.
Therefore, the number of vertices on
P_{i+1}
is
1
less than
P_{i}
if
\ell(\overline{v^{\prime}})=\ell(\underline{v^{\prime}})
as the level of a corresponding vertex cannot be lower (by examining
Maxlink
(V)
).
By Lemma
5.7
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
, the level of any vertex cannot be higher than
100\log\log n
.
As
P_{r}
has at most
10\log b
vertices, in round
R=20\log b\geq r+10\log b+100\log\log n
, the number of vertices on any
P_{R}
is at most
2
.
The lemma follows from a union bound.
∎

Lemma 5.17
.

Given graph
H
and integer
b\geq(\log n)^{100}
.
For any shortest path
P
in
H
such that there are at most
b^{7}
vertices on
P
, let
P_{R}
be defined in
PassivePathConstruction
(H,P)
.
Let path
P_{R+1}
be defined by replacing each vertex
v
on
P_{R}
with
v.p
in Step
3
of
Densify
(H,b)
, and let path
P_{\text{final}}
be defined by replacing each vertex
v
on
P_{R+1}
with
v.p
in Step
6
of
Densify
(H,b)
.
W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, there are at most
2
vertices on
P_{\text{final}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.16
, there are at most
2
vertices on path
P_{R}
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
.
The
Alter
(E(H))
in Step
3
of
Densify
(H,b)
replaces vertices by their parents in each path, so
P_{R+1}
has at most
2
vertices.
Since Step
5
of
Densify
(H,b)
only alters edges locally without changing
E_{\text{close}}
, the path
P_{R+1}
is unchanged. The following
Alter
(E_{\text{close}})
in Step
6
replaces each vertex on
P_{R+1}
by its parent.
As a result,
P_{\text{final}}
has at most
2
vertices.
∎

5.3
Increasing Vertex Degrees

In this section, we present the detailed algorithm
Increase
(V,E,b)
and prove that it increase the minimum degree of the current graph to at least
b
.

5.3.1
Algorithmic Framework of
Increase

During the execution of
Increase
(V,E,b)
, the subroutine
Densify
(H,b)
calls
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
(Step
1
of
Densify
(H,b)
) for
R=20\log b
times, where each
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
calls
Alter
(E)
for
2
times (Step
2
and Step
7
of
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
).

Increase
(V,E,b)
needs to use addition information of the execution, so we modify it as follows:

•

In Step
1
of
Densify
(H,b)
, pass the round id
i\in[0,R)
to
Expand-Maxlink
as an additional parameter.

•

Within
Expand-Maxlink
(H,i)
, in Step
2
, pass the integer
2i
to
Alter
(E)
as an additional parameter.

•

Within
Expand-Maxlink
(H,i)
, in Step
7
, pass the integer
2i+1
to
Alter
(E(V))
as an additional parameter.

•

For each
Alter
(E,j)
called within
Expand-Maxlink
(H,i)
, execute
v.p_{j}=v.p
for each vertex
v\in V(E)
.

•

In the
Alter
(E(H))
within Step
3
of
Densify
(H,b)
, execute
v.p_{2R}=v.p
for each vertex
v\in V(H)
.

•

In the
Alter
(E_{\text{close}})
within Step
6
of
Densify
(H,b)
, execute
v.p_{2R+1}=v.p
for each vertex
v\in V(E_{\text{close}})
.
The field
p_{j}
for all
j\in[0,2R+1]
is global.

All
v.p_{j}
for all
v\in V
and all
j\in[0,2R+1]
can be written in a cell indexed by
(v,j)
in the public RAM in
O(1)
using the processor corresponding to
v
, and the total space (and work) used for this part is at most
|V|\cdot O(R)\leq n/b^{10}\cdot O(\log n)
.
The modified
Alter
(E)
still takes
O(|E|)
work and
O(1)
time, so Lemma
5.10
still holds.

Definition 5.18
.

For any vertex
v\in V
, define
v.p^{(0)}\coloneqq v.p_{0}=v
,
and for any integer
j\in[2R+1]
, define
v.p^{(j)}\coloneqq(v.p^{(j-1)}).p_{j}
.

Increase
(V,E,b)
:

1.

Subgraph
H=(V,E^{\prime})=\textsc{Build}(V,E,b)
.

2.

(V,E_{\text{close}})=\textsc{Densify}(H,b)
.

3.

For each vertex
v\in V
: assign a block of
b^{9}
processors, which will be used as an indexed hash table
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v)
of size
b^{9}
.

4.

Choose a random hash function
h:[n]\to[b^{9}]
. For each vertex
v\in V
: compute
u\coloneqq v.p^{(2R+1)}
, then write
v
into the
h(v)
-th cell of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(u)
and update
v.p=u
.

5.

For each vertex
v\in V
: if the number of vertices in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v)
is at least
2b
then mark
v
as a
head
.

6.

For each non-loop edge
(v,w)\in E_{\text{close}}
: if
v
is a head and
w
is a non-head then
w.p=v
.

7.

Shortcut
(V)
.

8.

For each vertex
v\in V
: sample
v
as a
leader
w.p.
1/2
. For each non-loop edge
(v,w)\in E_{\text{close}}
: if
v
is a leader and
w
is a non-leader then
w.p.p=v.p
.

9.

Shortcut
(V)
.

10.

Alter
(E)
.

Lemma 5.19
.

W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{2}
,
Increase
(V,E,b)
runs in
O(\log b)
time and
O(m+n)
work on an ARBITRARY CRCW PRAM.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.1
, Step
1
takes
O(\log b)
time and
O(m+n)
work w.p.
1-n^{-8}
.
By Lemma
5.10
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{3}
, Step
2
takes
O(\log b)
time and
(m+n)/(\log n)^{3}
work.
Using approximate compaction (Lemma
4.2
), Step
3
takes
O(\log^{*}n)
time and
O(|V|\cdot b^{9})
work (to notify each indexed processor the block it belongs to) w.p.
1-n^{-9}
.

In Step
4
, each vertex in
V
chooses and stores a random number from
[b^{9}]
, giving function
h
, which takes
O(|V|)
works and
O(1)
time.
Next, the algorithm computes
u\coloneqq v.p^{(2R+1)}
for each
v\in V
. Since each
v.p_{j}
for each
v\in V
and each
j\in[0,2R+1]
is written in a cell indexed by
(v,j)
in the public RAM, we can compute
v.p^{(j)}
(
j\geq 1
) (by Definition
5.18
) for each
v
using the processor of
v
in
O(1)
time, given that
v.p^{(j-1)}
is stored in the private memory of
v
. Therefore,
u=v.p^{(2R+1)}
is computed iteratively in
O(R)=O(\log b)
time. The following hashing and parent update take
O(1)
time and
O(|V|)
work.
Therefore, Step
4
takes
O(|V|\cdot\log b)
works and
O(\log b)
time.

In Step
5
, we use the binary tree technique in the proof of Lemma
5.1
to count the number of occupied items in
\mathcal{H}(v)
for each
v
in
O(\log b)
time, which takes
O(\log b)
time and
O(|V|\cdot\log b)
work.
By the proof of Lemma
5.10
,
O(|E_{\text{close}}|)\leq O(m+n)/(\log n)^{4}
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{4}
, so Step
6
takes
O(m+n)/(\log n)^{4}
work and
O(1)
time w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{4}
.
Step
8
takes
O(|V|)+O(m+n)/(\log n)^{4}
work and
O(1)
time w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{4}
.
Step
7
and Step
9
take
O(|V|)
work and
O(1)
time.
14
14

14

Note that the total work performed from Step
2
to Step
9
is at most
O(m+n)/(\log n)^{3}+O(|V|\cdot b^{9})\leq(m+n)/(\log n)^{2.5}
w.p.
1-2/(\log n)^{3}
. In §
8
we will boost the success probability to
1-1/\text{poly}(n)
, which is obtained by running
\Theta(\log n)
instances of Step
2
to Step
9
in parallel (along with other techniques), so the overall work will be
O(m+n)
.

Step
10
takes
O(m)
work and
O(1)
time.
Summing up and by a union bound, the lemma follows.
∎

5.3.2
Properties of
Increase

Lemma 5.20
.

For any vertex
v\in V
, if
v.p^{(2R+1)}\neq v
then
v
is a non-root from Step
3
to Step
7
(inclusive) of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.

Proof.

Let
j\in[0,2R+1]
be minimum integer such that
v.p^{(j)}=(v.p^{(j-1)}).p_{j}\neq v
(Definition
5.18
),
then
v.p
is updated to a vertex
w\neq v
within the call to
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
in round
\lfloor j/2\rfloor
of Step
1
of
Densify
(H,b)
(if
j\leq 2R-1
) or within Step
5
of
Densify
(H,b)
(if
j=2R+1
), all within Step
2
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.
Recall that a non-root can never become a root during
Expand-Maxlink
(H)
or during Step
5
of
Densify
(H,b)
(
[
LTZ20
]
), so
v
is a non-root right after Step
2
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.
The parent updates in Step
4
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
cannot make
v
a root since
v.p^{(2R+1)}\neq v
.
The
Shortcut
(V)
in Step
7
cannot make a non-root into a root.
Moreover, the parent updates in Step
6
cannot make a non-root into a root either cause it only uses non-loop edges.
As a result,
v
must be a non-root from Step
3
to Step
7
(inclusive) of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.
∎

Lemma 5.21
.

W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
,
for any vertex
u\in V
, if there exists vertex
v\in V
such that
v.p^{(2R+1)}=u
then
u
is a root before Step
5
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.

Proof.

By Definition
5.18
,
u=(v.p^{2R}).p_{2R+1}
, where
p_{2R+1}
is the parent filed in Step
6
of
Densify
(H,b)
.
By Lemma
5.9
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, at the end of
Densify
(H,b)
, for any vertex
v\in V
,
v
is either a root or a child of a root, which also holds in Step
6
of
Densify
(H,b)
.
Since
u
is a parent of another vertex at that time,
u
must be a root.
So
u
is a root right after
Densify
(H,b)
(Step
2
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
).
Note that
u
cannot update its parent to a vertex
w\neq u
in Step
4
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.
Because otherwise,
u.p^{(2R+1)}=w\neq u
, making
u
a non-root after Step
2
by Lemma
5.20
, a contradiction.
Therefore,
u
remains to be a root after Step
4
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.
∎

Lemma 5.22
.

Given input graph
G=(V,E)
and parameter
b\geq(\log n)^{100}
, at the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{7}
, all vertices in
V
must be roots or children of roots and both ends of any edge in
E
must be roots.

Proof.

After Stage
1
, all trees are flat and both ends of any edge in
E
are roots (Lemma
4.21
).
By Lemma
5.9
,
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, at the end of
Densify
(H,b)
(Step
2
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
), for any vertex
v\in V
,
v
is either a root or a child of a root and both ends of any edge in
E_{\text{close}}
are roots. We shall condition on this.

By Lemma
5.21
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, if
v.p^{(2R+1)}=v
then
v
is a root before Step
4
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
and continues to be a root after this step.
By Lemma
5.20
and Lemma
5.21
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, if
v
updates its parent to a vertex
u\neq v
in Step
4
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
, then
v
is a non-root and
u
is a root before Step
4
, which means
v
is a child of a root and
u
is a root before executing
v.p=u
by the previous paragraph.
Therefore, each vertex in
V
is either a root or a child of a root after Step
4
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.

In Step
6
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
, a non-head updates its parent to be a head if possible, which makes each vertex in
V
a root, a child of a root, or a grandchild of a root.
The
Shortcut
(V)
in Step
7
makes all vertices in
V
roots or children of roots.

In Step
8
,
w.p.p
is updated to
v.p
if possible, which makes each vertex in
V
a root, a child of a root, or a grandchild of a root, because
w.p
and
v.p
are both roots by the previous paragraph.
The
Shortcut
(V)
in Step
9
makes all vertices in
V
roots or children of roots.

Now both ends of any edges in
E
are roots or children of roots.
So, the
Alter
(E)
in Step
10
makes all ends of all edges in
E
roots by the previous paragraph.
∎

Lemma 5.23
.

W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{7}
,
for any vertex
v\in V
, if
v.p^{(2R+1)}\neq v
then
v
is a non-root from Step
3
till the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.20
, if
v.p^{(2R+1)}\neq v
then
v
is a non-root from Step
3
to Step
7
(inclusive) of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.
By the last but two paragraph in the proof of Lemma
5.22
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{7}
, all vertices in
V
are roots or children of roots right after Step
7
.
So, the parent updates in Step
8
(
w.p.p=v.p
) can only update the parent of a root (
w.p
), which cannot turn a non-root into a root (which requires to update the parent of a non-root to itself).
∎

Lemma 5.24
.

Given input graph
G=(V,E)
and parameter
b\geq(\log n)^{100}
, let
H=\textsc{Build}(V,E,b)
be computed in Step
1
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.
W.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{6}
,
for any component
\mathcal{C}
in
H
such that
|\mathcal{C}|
(the number of vertices in
\mathcal{C}
) is less than
b^{6}
, at the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
, all vertices in
\mathcal{C}
must be roots or children of roots and all edges in
E
adjacent to these vertices must be loops.

Proof.

W.p.
1-n^{-8}
, assuming the statement in Lemma
5.4
always holds.
We get that
V(\mathcal{C})=V(\mathcal{C}_{G})
, where
\mathcal{C}_{G}
is the component in
G
induced on vertex set
V(\mathcal{C})
.

Note that for any shortest path
P
in
\mathcal{C}
, there are at most
b^{6}
vertices on
P
.
So by Lemma
5.16
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, any shortest path in
\mathcal{C}
at the end of Step
1
in
Densify
(H,b)
has length at most
1
, so any vertex pair in
\mathcal{C}
(with all edges in
E_{\text{close}}
) has distance at most
1
.
By the proof of Lemma
5.9
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, both ends of any edge in
E_{\text{close}}
are roots.
Also note that the
Alter
(E(H))
in Step
3
of
Densify
(H,b)
never increases the diameter of any component since it only replaces edges on any shortest path.
So, the component in graph
(V(E_{\text{close}}),E_{\text{close}})
corresponding to
\mathcal{C}
has diameter at most
1
and Theorem
2
is applicable.
After Step
5
of
Densify
(H,b)
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{9}
, for any such component
\mathcal{C}
,
the vertices in
\mathcal{C}
must be in the same tree (the algorithm in Theorem
2
runs independently on each component, so the success probability is not for all vertices in a fix
\mathcal{C}
, but for all vertices in all such
\mathcal{C}
), and these vertices must be roots or children of roots.

Consider the parent updates in Step
4
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.
By Lemma
5.20
and Lemma
5.21
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, if
v
updates its parent to a vertex
u\neq v
in Step
4
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
, then
v
is a non-root and
u
is a root before Step
4
.
By an induction on
j
from
0
to
2R+1
, one can show that
u=v.p^{(2R+1)}
is in the same component
\mathcal{C}
of
v
.
By the previous paragraph,
u,v
are in the same tree rooted at
u
and
v.p=u
before Step
4
, so the parent update does not change the parent of
v
.
On the other hand, if
v.p^{(2R+1)}=v
then
v
is a root before Step
4
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
by Lemma
5.21
and continues to be a root.
Therefore, the tree corresponding to any such component
\mathcal{C}
does not change during Step
4
.

By Lemma
5.9
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, the edge in
E_{\text{close}}
are only adjacent to roots before Step
4
.
By the previous paragraph, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, Step
4
never turns a root into a non-root.
So, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{7}
, the edge in
E_{\text{close}}
are only adjacent to roots before Step
6
.
Since any vertex pair
v,w
in
\mathcal{C}
are in the same tree, if there is an edge in
E_{\text{close}}
between
v,w
, the edge must be a loop.
So the parent updates in Step
6
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
do not change the tree corresponding to any such component
\mathcal{C}
, because only non-loop edges are used.

The
Shortcut
(v)
in Step
7
does not change the tree since all vertices in
\mathcal{C}
must be roots or children of roots.
Step
8
does not change the tree corresponding to any
\mathcal{C}
because all edges between vertices in
\mathcal{C}
remain to be loops.
The
Shortcut
(v)
in Step
9
does not change these trees either.
We shall prove the lemma by contradiction based on Lemma
5.22
: w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{7}
, both ends of any edge in
E
are roots at the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.

Assume for contradiction that there exists a component
\mathcal{C}
in
H
such that there exists an edge
(v,w)\in E
(where the edge set
E
here is already altered by the
Alter
(E)
in Step
10
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
) such that
v\neq w
at the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.
Then by the previous paragraph we know that both
v,w
are roots. Since
(v,w)\in E
, we have that
v,w
are in the same component
\mathcal{C}_{G}
.
15
15

15

We omit the proof that during the algorithm,
v,v.p
are in the same component of
G
and so do
v,w
if edge
(v,w)
presents in the current graph, which is by a standard induction by steps, e.g., see
[
LTZ20
]
or
[
LT22
]
for a proof.

By the second sentence of this proof,
V(\mathcal{C})=V(\mathcal{C}_{G})
.
So
v,w
must be in the same component
\mathcal{C}
.
By the previous paragraph,
v,w
must be within the same tree in the labeled digraph, contradicting with the fact that
v\neq w
and they are both roots.
As a result,
v=w
and thus all edges adjacent to vertices in
\mathcal{C}
must be loops.
The lemma follows from a union bound.
∎

Lemma
5.24
means that such small component (with size less than
b^{6}
) in
H
can be
ignored
in the rest of the entire algorithm as the connectivity computation on them is already
finished
.
In our presentation, we do not detect and delete all loops from the current graph, because although the loop-deletion does not affect the components that are already finished, it might decrease the spectral gap of other unfinished components, hurting the running time of our algorithm.

Our edge sampling in Stage
3
(§
6
) will operate on all edge including loops. Since each small component is contracted to
1
vertex,
16
16

16

Strictly speaking, such components are not fully contracted due to the existence of non-roots at the end of Stage
1
. The contraction will be done at the end of the algorithm, see footnote
12
.

the execution of our algorithm after Stage
2
does not affect such components, and it is equivalent to ignore all such components in the graph.

Lemma 5.25
.

Given input graph
G=(V,E)
and parameter
b\geq(\log n)^{100}
,
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{5}
,
for any root
v\in V
at the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
, the degree of
v
in the current graph is at least
b
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.24
and the discussion following it, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{6}
, any vertex in a component of
H
with size less than
b^{6}
is ignored in the rest of the algorithm since the computation of its component is finished.
Therefore, we assume that any vertex
v\in V
is in a component of
H
with size at least
b^{6}
.

Suppose any shortest path in
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
(Definition
5.3
) has less than
b^{6}
vertices on it.
Then, using the same argument in the proof of Lemma
5.24
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{6}
, all shortest paths in
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
have length at most
1
at the end of Step
3
of
Densify
(H,b)
, and the following Step
5
makes all vertices in
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
the root of a tree or the children of the root of this same tree. The parent updates after Step
2
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
do not change the tree corresponding to
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
by the same argument in the proof of Lemma
5.24
.
Let
u
be the root of the tree containing
v
.
By
|\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)|\geq b^{6}
, we get that the number of vertices
v\in V
with
v.p=u
is at least
b^{6}
(
u.p=u
also contributes
1
).
Since each vertex in
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
has at least one adjacent edge (otherwise it is a singleton and cannot be presented in a component of size at least
b^{6}
), it must have at least one adjacent edge in
G
, so the number of edges in
G
adjacent to vertices in
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
is at least
b^{6}/2\geq b
.
By the proof of Lemma
5.22
, all these edges are altered to be adjacent to root
u
at the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
, so the degree of
u
in the current graph is at least
b
.

Suppose there exists a shortest path in
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
with at least
b^{6}
vertices on it.
Consider a shortest path
P
in
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
starting at
v
such that
|P|\geq b^{6}/2
.
Such a path
P
must exist, otherwise any vertex
u\in\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
can travel to
v
then travel to any vertex
w\in\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
(by connectivity), giving a path of less than
b^{6}
vertices, a contradiction.
Recall that for each integer
i\in[0,|P|-1]
,
P(i)
is the
i
-th vertex on
P
.
For each integer
i\in[3,|P|-1]
, let path
P^{(i)}
be the subpath from
P(0)
to
P(i)
(inclusively) using edges on
P
, and let
P^{(i)}_{\text{final}}
be defined in Lemma
5.17
given
P^{(i)}
as the initial shortest path in
H
.
By Lemma
5.17
,
|P^{(i)}_{\text{final}}|\leq 2
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
for any shortest
P
and any integer
i\in[3,|P|-1]
.

Consider the (multi-)set of paths
\mathcal{P}\coloneqq\{P^{(i)}_{\text{final}}\mid i\in[3,|P|-1]\}
.
There are two cases.

1.

Suppose the set
\mathcal{P}
contains at most
b^{2}
distinct elements (paths). Note that each path in
\mathcal{P}
has form
(P(0).p^{(2R+1)},P(i).p^{(2R+1)})
for some
i\in[3,|P|-1]
by Definition
5.18
and the fact that all such paths have length at most
1
.
So, the vertex set

V_{P}\coloneqq\{P(0).p^{(2R+1)}\}\cup\{P(i).p^{(2R+1)})\mid i\in[3,|P|-1]\}

contains at most
b^{2}+1
distinct elements.
Observe that all vertices originally on path
P
are distinct since
P
is a shortest path.
Therefore, there exists a vertex
v^{*}\in V_{P}
such that at least

\frac{|P|-3}{b^{2}+1}\geq\frac{b^{6}/2-3}{b^{2}+1}\geq b^{2}

different
v^{\prime}
on
P
satisfies
v^{\prime}.p^{(2R+1)}=v^{*}
.
In Step
4
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
, each such vertex
v^{\prime}
writes itself to the
h(v^{\prime})
-th cell of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{*})
.
The probability that at most
2b
vertices are in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v^{*})
is at most

\binom{b^{9}}{2b}\cdot\left(\frac{2b}{b^{9}}\right)^{b^{2}}\leq b^{18b}\cdot b^{-7b^{2}}\leq b^{-b}\leq n^{-9}

by
b\geq(\log n)^{100}
.
Therefore, by a union bound over all vertices, w.p.
1-n^{-8}
, any of such vertex
v^{*}
is marked as a head in Step
5
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.

If
v\notin V_{P}
then there exists a vertex
u\neq v
such that
u\in V_{P}
and
v.p^{(2R+1)}=u
, which means
v
is a non-root w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{7}
by Lemma
5.23
, and the lemma holds for this case.
Now suppose
v\in V_{P}
, we have that
v=P(0).p^{(2R+1)}
by the fact that
v
is the first vertex on
P
.
There are two cases.

(a)

If
v=v^{*}
then the number of vertices
w\in\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
with
w.p^{(2R+1)}=v
is at least
b^{2}
, and all such
w
’s update their parents to
v
in Step
4
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.
Note that for any vertex
w
,
w.p^{(2R+1)}
is unique, so the number of children of
v
after (parallel execution of) Step
4
is at least
b^{2}
.
The parent updates in Step
6
can only increase the number of children of
v
or make
v
a non-root, because w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{7}
, the edge in
E_{\text{close}}
are only adjacent to roots before Step
6
by the fourth paragraph in the proof of Lemma
5.24
.
Suppose
v
is a root after Step
6
.
Step
7
can only increase the number of children of
v
since
v
is a root.
Step
8
can only increase the number of children of
v
or make
v
a non-root, because Step
7
makes all vertex in
V
roots or children of roots such that Step
8
can only update the parents of roots.
By the proof of Lemma
5.23
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{7}
, a non-root can never become a root,
thus if
v
is a root at the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
, then the degree of
v
is at least
b^{2}/2\geq b
by the same argument in the second paragraph of this proof.

(b)

If
v\neq v^{*}
then the path from
v
to
v^{*}
is an element in
\mathcal{P}
with path length
1
.
Therefore,
(v,v^{*})
is an edge in
E_{\text{close}}
after Step
2
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
and remains to be in
E_{\text{close}}
right before Step
6
.
If
v
is a head then the number of distinct vertices in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(v)
is at least
2b
by Step
5
, giving at least
2b
vertices whose parent is
v
(a root) by Step
4
, which continues to hold till Step
7
because Step
6
cannot make
v
a non-root nor remove any child from
v
(since any child of
v
has no adjacent edge). Step
8
can only increase the number of children of
v
or make
v
a non-root by the same argument in the previous case.
As a result, if
v
is a root at the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
then the degree of
v
is at least
b
by the same argument in the second paragraph of this proof.
Else if
v
is a non-head, since
v^{*}
is marked as a head w.p.
1-n^{-8}
,
v
becomes a non-root in Step
6
dues to edge
(v,v^{*})\in E_{\text{close}}
and remains so till the end of the algorithm, and the lemma holds for this case.

2.

Suppose the set
\mathcal{P}
contains at least
b^{2}
distinct elements (paths).
By definition, all paths (edges) in
\mathcal{P}
starts with
P(0).p^{(2R+1)}
.
Excluding the possible loop
(P(0).p^{(2R+1)},P(0).p^{(2R+1)})\in\mathcal{P}
, there are at least
b^{2}-1
paths with length
1
in
\mathcal{P}
, which correspond to at least
b^{2}-1
different non-loop edges in
E_{\text{close}}
at the end of Step
2
of
Increase
(V,E,b)
.
If
v
is a non-root before Step
8
, then w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{7}
,
v
is a non-root at the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
by the proof of Lemma
5.23
, and the lemma holds for this case.
Suppose
v
is a root before Step
8
, which gives
v=P(0).p^{(2R+1)}
by the argument in the previous case.
There are two cases.

(a)

Suppose
v
is sampled as a non-leader in Step
8
. The number of different non-loops edges
(v,w)
adjacent to
v
in
E_{\text{close}}
is at least
b^{2}-1
. So, by a Chernoff bound and
b\geq(\log n)^{100}
, w.p.
1-n^{-8}
, there exists a leader
w
. Since
v.p=p
, Step
8
updates the parent of the non-leader
v
to
w.p
.
If
v\neq w.p
then
v
is a non-root at the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
and the lemma holds for this case.
Else if
v=w.p
before Step
8
, then since
w
has an adjacent edge
(v,w)\in E_{\text{close}}
,
w
must be a root before Step
6
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{7}
(the fourth paragraph in the proof of Lemma
5.24
).
Since
w\neq v
and
w
updates its parent to
v
in Step
6
, we obtain that
v
is a head, and the lemma holds in this case by the proof in case
1b
.

(b)

Suppose
v
is sampled as a leader in Step
8
. The number of different non-loops edges
(v,w)
adjacent to
v
in
E_{\text{close}}
is at least
b^{2}-1
. By a Chernoff bound and
b\geq(\log n)^{100}
, w.p.
1-n^{-8}
, there exist
2b
non-leaders
w
such that
(v,w)\in E_{\text{close}}
and
w\neq v
.
So, after Step
8
, the number of different vertices
w
with
w.p.p=v.p=v
is at least
2b
.
After the
Shortcut
(V)
in Step
9
, the number of children of
v
is at least
2b
since
v
is a root.
Since each vertex in
\mathcal{C}_{H}(v)
has at least one adjacent edge (otherwise it is a singleton and cannot be presented in a component of size at least
b^{6}
), it must have at least one adjacent edge in
G
, so the number of edges in
G
adjacent to these (at least)
2b
different vertices (whose parent is
v
) is at least
2b/2=b
.
By the proof of Lemma
5.22
, all these edges are altered to be adjacent to root
v
at the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
, so the degree of
v
in the current graph is at least
b
.

By a union bound over all the bad events, the lemma follows.
∎

6
Stage 3: Connectivity on the Sampled Graph

After Stage
2
, by Lemma
5.25
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{6}
, we can assume
\deg(G)\geq b
, where
G
is the current graph at the end of
Increase
(V,E,b)
and
b=(\log n)^{100}
.

We also assume that the minimum spectral gap
\lambda
over all connected components
(henceforth also called the
component-wise spectral gap
)
of the original input graph satisfies

\lambda\geq b^{-0.1}=(\log n)^{-10}.

This assumption will be removed in §
7
to give a connected components algorithm for any graphs with any spectral gap and the algorithm does not need any prior knowledge on the spectral gap.

The following lemma states that Stages 1 and 2 of the algorithm does not decrease the component-wise spectral gap of the original graph.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\lambda
be the component-wise spectral gap of the original input graph.
The component-wise spectral gap
\lambda_{G}
of the current graph
G
at the end of Stage
2
satisfies that
\lambda_{G}\geq\lambda
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.21
, the algorithm in Stage
1
(
Reduce
(V,E,k)
) is a contraction algorithm since all vertices
v
are contracted to vertex
v.p
, so the component-wise spectral gap of the graph at the end of Stage
1
is at least
\lambda
since a contraction can only increase the spectral gap of any graph (
[
CG97
]
Chapter 1.4, Lemma 1.15).

The algorithm in Stage
2
(
Increase
(V,E,b)
) might create trees of height
2
dues to the existence of non-roots created in Stage
1
.
By Lemma
5.22
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, all edges in the current graph are only adjacent on roots and the algorithm never deletes edges nor add edges (but only replace them by replacing ends with their parents), so all vertices in a tree rooted at
v
are contracted to
v
, giving a contraction algorithm
Increase
(V,E,b)
for Stage
2
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
.
As a result, the component-wise spectral gap
\lambda_{G}
of the current graph
G
at the end of Stage
2
is at least
\lambda
w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
(this probability will be boosted to high-probability in §
8
).
17
17

17

As an alternative proof, one can apply Cheeger’s inequality for non-simple graph (
[
Chu96
]
) and note that a contraction can only increase the graph conductance since it removes candidate cuts. This would give
\lambda_{G}\geq\lambda^{3}
that suffices for our use.

∎

The algorithm below, called

SampleSolve
(G=(V,E))
, computes the connected components of a graph given a promised component-wise spectral gap.
Essentially, the algorithm runs Stages 1 and 2, at the end of which all vertices have sufficiently large degree (except for the ones in tiny components which we can ignore). At this moment,
we downsample the number of edges
by a
1/\poly\log n
factor, and then run
[
LTZ20
]
.
In this section, we prove that the algorithm achieves
O(\log\log n)
time and linear expected work with good probability. We remove the known spectral gap assumption in §
7
and boost the guarantees to high probability in §
8
.

SampleSolve
(G=(V,E))
:

1.

If
|V|\leq n^{0.1}
then removes parallel edges and loops from
E
and compute the connected components of graph
(V,E)
by Theorem
2
and goto Step
4
.

2.

Sample each edge in
E
w.p.
1/(\log n)^{7}
to obtain graph
G^{\prime}
.

3.

Compute the connected components of graph
G^{\prime}
by Theorem
2
.

4.

For each vertex
v
in the original input graph:
v.p=v.p.p.p
.

Theorem 3
.

There is an ARBITRARY CRCW PRAM algorithm that computes the connected components of any given graph with component-wise spectral gap
\lambda\geq(\log n)^{-10}
in
O(\log\log n)
time and
O(m)+\overline{O}(n)
work w.p.
1-1/(\log n)
.

Proof.

We run algorithm
SampleSolve
(G=(V,E))
where
G
is the current graph at the end of Stage
2
.

By Lemma
4.21
, all vertices at the end of Stage
1
are roots or children of roots, as required by Stage
2
.
By Lemma
5.22
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, all vertices in
V
must be roots or children of roots, where
V
is the set of roots at the end of Stage
1
. Therefore, all vertices in the input graph are in trees of height at most
2
.
Within
SampleSolve
(G)
in Stage
3
, either in its Step
1
or Step
3
, the contraction algorithm in Theorem
2
makes each root at the beginning of Stage
3
a root or a child of root at the end of Step
3
, all vertices in the input graph are in trees of height at most
3
.
Step
4
of
SampleSolve
(G)
makes all trees flat at the end of Stage
3
.

By Lemma
4.25
, Stage
1
runs in
O(\log\log n)
time and
O(m)+\overline{O}(n)
work w.p.
1-n^{-2}
, and
|V|\leq n/b^{10}
as required by Stage
2
.
By Lemma
5.19
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{2}
, Stage
2
takes
O(\log b)=O(\log\log n)
time and
O(m+n)
work.
So, the first two stages take
