.

8
Boosting the Work and Success Probability

In this section we improve the total work of our algorithm to
O(m+n)
and the success probability to high probability in
n
.

8.1
Bounding the Size of Edge Set with High Probability

The main step towards getting a high probability bound is to show that in the execution of
Extract
(E,k)
, the size of each
E_{i}
is bounded by
2\cdot 0.999^{i-1}\cdot n
with high probability for
i\in[1,k]
. As we discussed in the proof of Lemma
4.15
in order for some edge
(v,u)
to contribute to
E_{i+1}
through a vertex
v
, all of the edges originally in
E_{i,v}
must be deleted by the end of the
Filter
(E^{\prime},k)
. We show that the excepted number of edges that contribute to
E_{i+1}
is concentrated around its mean. The main challenge to provide a concentration bound is that the events that two different edges
e,e^{\prime}\in E_{i}
contribute to
E_{i+1}
are not necessarily independent from each other. For example, suppose that
e=(v,u)
and
e^{\prime}=(v,u^{\prime})
. In this case
e
and
e^{\prime}
share a common endpoint and if we condition on the event that
e
contributes to
E_{i+1}
, then it implies that all of the edges in
E_{i,v}
or all edges in
E_{i,u}
are deleted within
Filter
. This intuitively increases the probability that
e^{\prime}
contributes to the
E_{i+1}
since if all edges in
E_{i,v}
are deleted, then
e^{\prime}
also has a good chance contributes to the matching. The reason that we cannot say that
e^{\prime}
contributes to
E_{i+1}
with the probability
1
, is that
e^{\prime}
might have moved to some other vertex and that vertex might have some edges that are not deleted by the end of
Filter
. This however, still shows a possible positive correlation between the contribution of vertices, and we can’t simply use Chernoff-like concentration bounds as there are only valid for random variables with negative association.

In order to prove the concentration bound, we take two steps. First we show that for all rounds
i
and vertices
v\in V^{\prime}_{i}
if
|E_{i,v}|\geq 1000\log n
, then with the high probability no edges contribute to
E_{i+1}
through these vertices. Therefore, we can assume that for every vertex
v
such that some edge contributes to
E_{i+1}
through
v
we have
|E_{i,v}|<1000\log n
, thus the total contribution of
v
is relatively small. We then use this observation, and apply the concentration bound for self-bounding functions to show that
E_{i}
is bounded by
2\cdot 0.999^{i-1}\cdot n
with high probability.

Consider any edge
e\in E_{i}
, the probability that this edge gets deleted by the end of
Filter
(E^{\prime},k)
is
(1-(1-10^{-4})^{k+1})
. Since we run the algorithm for
k=1000\log\log\log n
, then we have the following bound for the probability that
e
gets deleted by the end of the
Filter
(E^{\prime},k)
.

\displaystyle(1-(1-10^{-4})^{k+1})=(1-(1-10^{-4})^{1000\log\log\log n+1})\leq 1-\frac{1}{\log\log n}.

Now suppose that for a vertex
v\in V^{\prime}_{i}
we have
|E_{i,v}|\geq 1000\log n
, then the probability that all of the edges in
|E_{i,v}|
gets deleted is at most

\displaystyle(1-\frac{1}{\log\log n})^{1000\log n}\leq n^{-10}.

Therefore, we can say that if for a vertex
v
, we have
|E_{i,v}|\geq 1000\log n
, then no edges will contribute to
E_{i+1}
through
v
with the probability of
(1-n^{-10})
. By taking the union bound over all possible
n
different vertices
v
and
k
rounds of
Extract
(E,k)
, we can say that with the probability of at least
(1-n^{-9}k)\leq(1-n^{-8})
, for all rounds
i\in[0,k-1]
if a vertex
v\in V^{\prime}_{i}
has more than
1000\log n
edges in
E_{i,v}
, then none of these edges contribute to
E_{i+1}
through
v
.

For a round
i
of
Extract
(E,k)
, let
V^{\prime\prime}_{i}
be the set of vertices in
V_{i}
such that for each
v\in V^{\prime\prime}_{i}
we have
|E_{i,v}|<1000\log n
. As we discussed, all of the contribution to
E_{i+1}
are through vertices in
V^{\prime\prime}_{i}
with high probability in
n
. This means that with high probability in
n
, the total contribution of all vertices to
E_{i+1}
is at most
1000\log n
. We then use this property, and apply the concentration bounded for self-bounding functions. First we define the self-bounding functions.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{n}
be
n
independent random variables taking values in some measure space
\mathcal{X}
. Also, let
f:\mathcal{X}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, then
f
is
(a)
-self-bounding if there exists functions
f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{n}:\mathcal{X}^{n-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle 0\leq f(x)-f_{j}(x^{(j)})\leq 1\,.

And,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(f(x)-f_{j}(x^{(j)})\big)\leq a\cdot f(x)\,.

For all inputs
x=(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
where
x^{(j)}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{j-1},x_{j+1},\cdots,x_{n})
is the
x
when
j^{\text{th}}
element is dropped.

The following concentration bound is known for self-bounding functions

Lemma 8.2

(
[
BLM09
]
)
.

Let
Z=f(X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{n})
, then if
f(.)
is
(a)
-self-bounding function we have

\displaystyle\Pr[Z\geq\E[Z]+t]\leq\exp\bigg(\frac{-t^{2}}{2a\E[Z]+a\cdot t}\bigg)\,.

We now use the concentration bound above, to provide a concentration bound for
|E_{i+1}|
. Let us fix an ordering on the edges in
E_{i}
and assume that
|E_{i}|=\{e_{1},e_{2},\cdots,e_{\ell}\}
where
\ell=|E_{i}|
. Also, let
X_{e_{1}},X_{e_{2}},\cdots,X_{e_{\ell}}
be
\ell
random variables where
X_{e_{j}}
indicates whether
e_{j}
is deleted by the end of the
Filter
(E^{\prime},k)
. Specifically,
X_{e_{j}}=1
if
e_{j}
is deleted by
Filter
, and it is
0
otherwise. It is then clear that these random variables are independent as
Filter
deletes each edge independent of others. Now we define function
f_{v}:\{0,1\}^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as follows for every
v\in V^{\prime\prime}_{i}
.

f_{v}(x)=\begin{cases}|E_{i,v}|,&\text{If for all edges $e\in E_{i,v}$ we have $x_{e}=1$}\\
&\text{, i.e., all edges in $E_{i,v}$ are deleted based on the input $x$}\\
0,&\text{Otherwise}\end{cases}

and we define function
f
as follows
f(x)=\frac{\sum_{v\in V^{\prime\prime}_{i}}f_{v}(x)}{2000\log n}
. We then claim that
f
is
(1000\log n)
-self-bounding.

Claim 8.3
.

Let
f
be the function defined as above, then
f
is
(1000\log n)
-self-bounding.

Proof.

For each
e_{j}\in E_{i}
, we define function
f_{e_{j}}(x^{(e_{j})})
as below.

\displaystyle f_{e_{j}}(x^{(e_{j})})=f(x_{e_{1}},\cdots,x_{e_{i-1}},0,x_{e_{i+1}},\cdots,x_{e_{\ell}})\,.

In other words,
f_{e_{j}}(x^{(e_{j})})
is the same as function
f
when we change that
e_{j}
is not deleted. For each
e_{j}
, we use
x^{\prime}_{j}
to denote the input sequence
(x_{e_{1}},\cdots,x_{e_{i-1}},0,x_{e_{i+1}},\cdots,x_{e_{\ell}})
. Thus, it is equivalent to define
f_{e_{j}}(x^{(e_{j})})=f(x^{\prime}_{j})
. Also, for an input sequence
x
, we use
\eta(x)
to denote the set of vertices in
V^{\prime\prime}_{i}
such that for each
v\in\eta(x)
all edges in
E_{i,v}
are deleted according to
x
. Based on this definition, we can re-write function
f
as follows.

\displaystyle f(x)=\frac{\sum_{v\in\eta(x)}|E_{i,v}|}{2000\log n}

Now we are ready to show these function
f
satisfies the properties of self-bounding functions. Considering the first property we have to show that for every edge
e_{j}
we have

\displaystyle 0\leq f(x)-f_{e_{j}}(x^{(e_{j})})\leq 1

Let
e_{j}=(v^{\prime},u^{\prime})
. It is then clear that
\eta({x^{\prime}_{j}})\subseteq\eta(x)
since in
x^{\prime}
we are changing the input such that
e_{j}
is not deleted, and it is only changed for one edge. Then by the definition of functions
f(.)
and
f_{e_{j}}(.)
we have

\displaystyle f(x)-f_{e_{j}}(x^{(e_{j})})

\displaystyle=f(x)-f(x^{\prime}_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{v\in\eta(x),v\notin\eta({x^{\prime}_{j}})}|E_{i,v}|}{2000\log n}

(7)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|E_{i,v^{\prime}}|+|E_{i,u^{\prime}}|}{2000\log n}

\displaystyle(\text{$\eta({x^{\prime}_{j}})$ is different from $\eta(x)$ for at most vertices adjacent to $e_{j}$})

\displaystyle\leq 1\,.

\displaystyle(\text{Since $|E_{i,v}|\leq 1000\log n$ for every vertex $v\in V^{\prime\prime}_{i}$ })

Thus, function
f
satisfies the first property of self-bounding functions.

For the second property we have

\displaystyle\sum_{e_{j}\in E_{i}}f(x)-f_{e_{j}}(x^{(e_{j})})

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{j}\in E_{i}}\bigg(\frac{\sum_{v\in\eta(x),v\notin\eta({x^{\prime}_{j}})}|E_{i,v}|}{2000\log n}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{v\in\eta(x)}\bigg(\frac{1000\log n\cdot|E_{i,v}|}{2000\log n}\bigg)

\displaystyle(\text{$\eta({x^{\prime}_{j}})$ is different from $\eta(x)$ for at most vertices adjacent to $e_{j}$}

\displaystyle\text{and each $v\in V^{\prime\prime}_{i}$ is adjacent to at most $1000\log n$ edges})

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\eta(x)}\bigg(\frac{|E_{i,v}|}{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=1000\log n\cdot f(x)\,.

Thus, function
f
is
(1000\log n)
-self-bounded and it proof the claim.
∎

We now show the concentration bound for
E_{i+1}
. Recall that
X_{e_{1}},X_{e_{2}},\cdots,X_{e_{\ell}}
are the random variables where
X_{e_{i}}
indicates whether
e_{i}
is deleted by the end of the
Filter
. Also, let
\vec{X}=(X_{e_{1}},X_{e_{2}},\cdots,X_{e_{\ell}})
. First we claim that with the probability of
(1-n^{-8})
we have
|E_{i+1}|\leq\sum_{v\in V^{\prime\prime}_{i}}f_{v}(\vec{X})
. The reason is that in order an edge
(v,u)
contribute to
E_{i+1}
, for at least one of its endpoints, let’s say
v
, all the edges in
E_{i,v}
must be deleted. In this case
f_{v}(\vec{X})
is
E_{i,v}
which is an upper bound on the number of edges that contribute to
E_{i+1}
through
v
. Also with the probability of at least
(1-n^{-8})
all of the contribution to
E_{i+1}
are through vertices in
V^{\prime\prime}_{i}
. This implies that
|E_{i+1}|\leq\sum_{v\in V^{\prime\prime}_{i}}f_{v}(\vec{X})
, and
\frac{|E_{i+1}|}{2000\log n}\leq f(\vec{X})
.

Also, as we discussed in the proof of Lemma
4.15
, the expected number of edges contributed through each vertex
v\in V^{\prime}_{i}
is upper bounded by
1
. Using the similar argument we can say that
\E[f_{v}(\vec{X})]\leq 1
for each
v\in V^{\prime\prime}_{i}
. Thus,
\E[f(\vec{X})]\leq\frac{|V^{\prime\prime}_{i}|}{2000\log n}
. Let
Z=f(\vec{X})
. Since
f
is
(1000\log n)
-self-bounded function, using the concentration bound for self-bounding functions we can say

\displaystyle\Pr[Z\geq\E[Z]+n^{0.6}]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigg(\frac{-n^{1.2}}{2000\log n\E[Z]+1000\log n\cdot n^{0.6}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigg(\frac{-n^{1.2}}{|V^{\prime\prime}_{i}|+1000\log n\cdot n^{0.6}}\bigg)

\displaystyle(\text{Since $\E[Z]\leq\frac{|V^{\prime\prime}_{i}|}{2000\log n}$})

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigg(\frac{-n^{1.2}}{n+1000\log n\cdot n^{0.6}}\bigg)\leq n^{-10}

\displaystyle(\text{Since $|V^{\prime\prime}_{i}|\leq n$}).

Recall that with the probability of at least
(1-n^{-8})
we had
\frac{|E_{i+1}|}{2000\log n}\leq f(\vec{X})
. By combining this with the concentration bound above, we can say with the probability of at least
(1-n^{-7})
we have

\displaystyle\frac{|E_{i+1}|}{2000\log n}\leq\E[f(\vec{X})]+n^{0.6}

Thus,

\displaystyle|E_{i+1}|

\displaystyle\leq 2000\log n\cdot E[f(\vec{X})]+2000\log n\cdot n^{0.6}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V^{\prime\prime}_{i}}E[f_{v}(\vec{X})]+2000\log n\cdot n^{0.6}

\displaystyle\leq|V^{\prime\prime}_{i}|+2000\log n\cdot n^{0.6}\,.

By Lemma
4.12
, with the probability of at least
1-kn^{-9}\geq 1-n^{-8}
, we have
|V^{\prime\prime}_{i}|\leq 0.999^{i}\cdot n
. Thus with the probability of at least
1-n^{-6}
we have

\displaystyle|E_{i+1}|\leq|V^{\prime\prime}_{i}|+2000\log n\cdot n^{0.6}\leq 2\cdot 0.999^{i}\cdot n\,,

which proves the following lemma.

Lemma 8.4
.

For each round
i\in[1,k]
of
Extract
, with the probability of at least
1-n^{-6}
we have
|E_{i}|\leq 2\cdot 0.999^{i-1}\cdot n
.

We can use the same technique to provide the high probability bound for the size of
E^{\prime}
in the
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
.

Corollary 8.5
.

Let
E^{\prime}
be the set of edges by end of Step
8
of
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
. Then,
|E^{\prime}|\leq 10^{9}\cdot n
with the probability of at least
1-n^{-6}
.

Proof.

The set
E^{\prime}
is computed in the same way that
E_{i+1}
is computed in round
i
of
Extract
. The only difference is that in the proof above, we are assuming that each edge gets deleted with the probability of
(1-10^{-4})
for
1000\log\log\log n
rounds. However,
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
this happens for every edge for
10^{6}\cdot 1.1^{i}\log\log n
rounds. Also, it is guaranteed that
10^{6}\cdot 1.1^{i}\log\log n
is bounded by
10^{6}\cdot(\log n)^{3}
. We show that we can still claim that in order for a vertex to have a contribution to
E^{\prime}
, it should have at most
10^{8}\cdot(\log n)^{4}
edges. Consider a vertex with at least
10^{8}\cdot(\log n)^{4}
edges. Then, the probability that all of these edges get deleted is

\displaystyle(1-(1-10^{-4})^{10^{6}\cdot 1.1^{i}\log\log n})^{10^{8}\cdot(\log n)^{4}}\leq(1-(1-10^{-4})^{10^{6}\cdot(\log n)^{3}})^{10^{8}\cdot(\log n)^{4}}\leq n^{-10}\,.

Thus, with high probability in
n
, vertices with more than
10^{8}\cdot(\log n)^{4}
edges do not contribute to
E^{\prime}
. Then we define the function
f=\frac{\sum_{v\in V^{\prime\prime}}f_{v}(x)}{2\cdot 10^{8}\cdot(\log n)^{4}}
where
V^{\prime\prime}
is the set of vertices with at most
10^{8}\cdot(\log n)^{4}
edges. Then, it is straightforward to verify that
f
is
(10^{8}\cdot(\log n)^{4})
-self bounding and get the concentration bound stated in this corollary.
∎

We further leverage this technique to get the concentration bound for the set of edges
E^{\prime}
in Step
4
of
Reduce
. The proof immediately follows from what we have discussed so far as it calls
Filter
with
k=10^{6}\log\log n
.

Corollary 8.6
.

Let
E^{\prime}
be the set of edges by end of Step
4
of
Reduce
. Then,
|E^{\prime}|\leq 10^{7}\cdot n
with the probability of at least
1-n^{-6}
.

8.2
Boosting to Linear Work with High Probability

We show that
Connectivity
(G)
can be implemented to take
O(m+n)
work with high probability.

Lemma 8.7
.

For any graph
G
, w.p.
1-1/n
,
Connectivity
(G)
takes
O(m+n)
work.

Proof.

Consider the proof of Lemma
7.17
.
Step
2
of
Connectivity
(G)
takes
O(m)+\overline{O}(n)
work because the edge set
E^{\prime}
in Step
4
of
Reduce
(V,E,k)
has
\E[|E^{\prime}|]\leq\overline{O}(n)
.
Using the technique in §
8.1
, we can show that
|E^{\prime}|\leq O(n)
w.p.
1-n^{-6}
, giving
O(m+n)
total work for Step
2
of
Connectivity
(G)
w.p.
1-n^{-6}
.

It remains to improve the total work of Step
5
of
Connectivity
(G)
to
O(m+n)
with high probability.
Consider the
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
in each phase
i\in[0,10\log\log n)
.
In the proof of Lemma
7.17
, we already showed that the total work of Step
6
to Step
10
of
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
over all phases is at most
O(m+n)
w.p.
1-n^{-2}
.

Observe that Step
2
and Step
3
take
2(m+n)/(\log n)^{2}
work w.p.
1-1/\log n
.
The idea is to run
\log n
instances of Step
2
and Step
3
in parallel independently, and halt the execution of an instance when the work of that execution reaches
2(m+n/(\log n)^{2})
, so the total work would be
2(m+n)/(\log n)^{2}\cdot\log n
w.p.
1-n^{-9}
.
To implement
\log n
instances of parallel execution, note that
|E(H_{1})|,|E(H_{2})|\leq m/(\log n)^{6}
w.p.
1-n^{-8}
.
At the end of Step
3
of
Connectivity
(G)
, we approximate compact the arrays of edges of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
into arrays of length at most
2m/(\log n)^{6}
in
O(\log^{*}n)
time and
O(m)
work w.p.
1-n^{-9}
.
Since the edge of
H_{1},H_{2}
are indexed, at the beginning of each phase we can use
4m/(\log n)^{5}
processors to make
\log n
copies of
H_{1},H_{2}
.
Observe from the proof of Lemma
7.8
, Lemma
7.13
, and Lemma
5.10
, that each atomic step of Step
2
and Step
3
in
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
uses
O(|V(G^{\prime})|+|E(H_{1})|+|E(H_{2})|)\leq(m+n)/(\log n)^{5}
processors w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{2}
, so we can use
(m+n)/(\log n)^{4}
processors for the
\log n
independent parallel executions.
The same observation implies that Step
2
and Step
3
perform more than
2(m+n/(\log n)^{2})
work only if the algorithm wants to use more than
(m+n)/(\log n)^{5}
processors in an atomic step (e.g., when assigning block of processors to a vertex with level more than
100\log\log n
), which can be detected and we halt the execution of that instance.
Finally, for all instances of Step
2
and Step
3
that finishes its execution without halting, pick an arbitrary one to continue the execution of
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
and discard the others.
As a result, Step
2
and Step
3
over all the
10\log\log n
phases takes
O(m+n)
work w.p.
1-n^{-8}
.

To bound the total work of Step
4
, we only need to show that Step
4
of
Remain
(G^{\prime},H_{1})
takes
O(n)
work w.p.
1-n^{-8}
by the last paragraph in the proof of Lemma
7.17
.
Since
|E_{\text{remain}}|\leq n/(\log n)^{6}
w.p.
1-n^{-9}
, we run
\log n
instances of the algorithm in Theorem
2
in parallel and halt the execution of the instance if it reaches
n/(\log n)^{5}
using the technique described in the previous paragraph.
As a result, Step
4
of
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
takes
O(n)
work w.p.
1-n^{-8}
.
The lemma follows from a union bound.
∎

Next, we show that our algorithm computes the connected components in desired time with high probability using the implementation in the proof of Lemma
8.7
.

Lemma 8.8
.

For any graph
G
with component-wise spectral gap
\lambda
, w.p.
1-1/n
,
Connectivity
(G)
computes all the connected components of
G
in
O(\log(1/\lambda)+\log\log n)
time.

Proof.

In the proofs of Lemma
7.21
and Lemma
8.7
, we already showed that Step
1
, Step
2
, Step
3
, Step
4
, and Step
6
can be implemented to take
O(\log\log n)
time w.p.
1-2/n^{-2}
.
It remains to prove for Step
5
of
Connectivity
(G)
.

In the proof of Lemma
7.18
, we showed that Step
2
and Step
3
of
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
take
O(\log b)
time w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{2}
.
In the proof of Lemma
8.7
, we run
\log n
parallel instances of Step
2
and Step
3
of
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
.
So we can halt the execution of an instance if it reaches
c\log n
running time where
c>0
is an absolute constant such that Step
2
and Step
3
take at most
c\log b
time.
Therefore, the statement in Lemma
7.18
holds w.p.
1-n^{-2}
.

In the proof of Lemma
7.19
, we used the fact that w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{8}
, Step
3
of the algorithm
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
is a contraction algorithm on
H_{1}
.
One can detect whether this step is a contraction algorithm on
H_{1}
by checking whether all vertices in
V(H_{1})
are either roots or children of roots.
In the proof of Lemma
8.7
, we run
\log n
parallel instances of Step
2
and Step
3
of
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
.
So we can discard an instance at the end of the execution if it is not a contraction algorithm on
H_{1}
.
Therefore, Step
3
is a contraction algorithm on
H_{1}
w.p.
1-n^{-9}
.
In
Remain
(G^{\prime},H_{1})
in Step
4
, its first
3
steps take
O(\log^{*}n)
time w.p.
1-n^{-9}
.
In the proof of Lemma
8.7
,
we run
\log n
instances of the algorithm in Theorem
2
in parallel.
So we halt the execution of the instance if it reaches
c(\log d+\log\log n)
time where
c
is an absolute constant, which happens w.p. at most
1/(\log n)^{9}
.
As a result, the statement in Lemma
7.19
holds w.p.
1-n^{-7}
.

In the proof of Lemma
7.20
, we proved that in the last phase
i
, w.p.
1-1/(\log n)^{4}
, all edges of
H_{1}
are loops at the end of Step
3
of
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
, which can be detected using
|E(H_{1})|\leq m/(\log n)^{6}
processors in
O(1)
time w.p.
1-n^{-8}
.
In the proof of Lemma
8.7
, we run
\log n
parallel instances of Step
2
and Step
3
, so w.p.
1-n^{-9}
, at least one of the instance at the end of its execution satisfies that all edges of
H_{1}
are loops and we choose this one to continue the execution of
Interweave
(G^{\prime},H_{1},H_{2},E_{\text{filter}},i)
and discard the others.
Therefore, the statement in Lemma
7.20
holds w.p.
1-n^{-7}
.

Since Lemma
7.18
, Lemma
7.19
, and Lemma
7.20
hold w.p.
1-1/n
, the rest of the proof is the same as the proof of Lemma
7.21
.
∎

By Lemma
8.7
and Lemma
8.8
, Theorem
1
follows immediately.

Appendix A
Conditional Lower Bound

In this section, we show that our time bound is optimal when the input graph has component-wise spectral gap at most
1/\poly(\log(n))
conditioning on the widely believed

2-Cycle
Conjecture (see below).
The approach is similar to that of
[
BDE+19
]
, who gives an
\Omega(\log d)
conditional lower bound, where
d\geq\poly(\log(n))
is the diameter.

Conjecture A.1

(
2-Cycle
Conjecture
[
RVW18
,
ASW19
,
ASS+18
,
BDE+19
]
)
.

Any MPC algorithm that uses
n^{1-\Omega(1)}
space per processor requries
\Omega(\log n)
MPC rounds to distinguish between one cycle of size
n
from two cycles of size
n/2
w.h.p.

For simplicity, we state the theorem in the MPC model so that our proof can be based on the MPC model.
The conditional lower bound easily holds in any PRAM model with
O(m+n)
total memory by a simple simulation
[
ASW19
,
ASS+18
]
: if there exists a PRAM algorithm that distinguishes between one cycle from two cycles in
o(\log n)
time, then an MPC with
n^{1-\Omega(1)}
space per processor can simulate this PRAM algorithm with
O(1)
overhead, which violates the conjecture.

Theorem 4
.

If the
2-Cycle
Conjecture is true, then there exists a constant
c>20
such that any MPC algorithm with
n^{1-\Omega(1)}
space per processor that w.h.p. computes connected components of any given
n
-vertex graph with component-wise spectral gap
\lambda^{\prime}
requires
\Omega(\log(1/\lambda^{\prime}))
rounds, where
\lambda^{\prime}\leq\log^{-c}n
is a fixed parameter.

For the contradiction, we assume that there exists an MPC algorithm
ALG
with
n^{1-\Omega(1)}
space per processor that w.h.p. computes connected components of any given
n
-vertex graph with component-wise spectral gap
\lambda^{\prime}
in
o(\log(1/\lambda^{\prime}))
rounds, where
\lambda^{\prime}
is given in Theorem
4
.

Given a graph
G
which consists a cycle of size
n
or two cycles of size
n/2
.
The algorithm proceeds in rounds.
In each round, delete each edge of
G
w.p.
\lambda^{\prime 1/4}
independently to get graph
G^{\prime}
.
Compute the connected components of
G^{\prime}
by
ALG
.
Contract each connected components of
G^{\prime}
to a single vertex in
G
and add the edges deleted in this round to
G
, completing this round.
Repeat for
R
rounds until the number of edges in
G
is
n^{o(1)}
such that
G
can be stored in one processor and solved where
R
is a parameter to be tuned.
We need the following claim.

Lemma A.2
.

The graph
G
at the beginning of each round and subgraph
G^{\prime}
during each round is a collection of paths and cycles.

Proof.

The statement is trivially true at the beginning of the algorithm.
By an induction, assuming the graph
G
at the beginning of each round is a collection of paths and cycles.
During this round,
G^{\prime}
is a subgraph of
G
after edge deletions, which is still a collection of paths and cycles (singleton vertex is considered as a path or a cycle of size
1
, where
size
is defined as the number of vertices).
After contracting connected components of
G^{\prime}
, the resulting
G
is also a collection of paths and cycles, completing the induction.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Each round of the algorithm takes
o(\log(1/\lambda^{\prime}))
MPC rounds w.h.p.

Proof.

The edge deletion at the beginning of the round and the contraction at the end of the round can be implemented in
O(1)
MPC rounds (e.g., see
[
ASS+18
]
).
It remains to show that the graph
G^{\prime}
(
G
after edge deletions) has spectral gap at least
\lambda^{\prime}
w.h.p. at the beginning of each round.
22
22

22

If graph
G^{\prime}
has component-wise spectral gap greater than
\lambda^{\prime}
, the algorithm creates a
dummy
component of spectral gap
\lambda^{\prime}
and adds it into
G^{\prime}
such that
G^{\prime}
has component-wise spectral gap exactly
\lambda^{\prime}
and thus
ALG
is applicable on
G^{\prime}
. The dummy component has no edge with the original
G^{\prime}
so does not influence the computation of other components. Moreover, the dummy component has at most
m
edges (e.g. a path of fixed length) and can be created and deleted at the end of this round in
O(1)
MPC rounds.

By Lemma
A.2
,
G^{\prime}
is a collection of paths and cycles.
In the following, we show that w.h.p. the size of each path and cycle is at most
\lambda^{\prime-1/3}
, then a union bound gives the result since there are at most
n
such paths and cycles.
Fix a path/cycle of size
\lambda^{\prime-1/3}+1
, the number of edges in it is at least
\lambda^{\prime-1/3}
.
The probability that none of these edges gets deleted in this round is at most

\left(1-\lambda^{\prime 1/4}\right)^{\lambda^{\prime-1/3}}\leq\left(1-\lambda^{\prime 1/4}\right)^{\lambda^{\prime-1/4}\cdot 5\log n}\leq n^{-3}

by
\lambda^{\prime}\leq\log^{-c}n
and
c>20
.
By a union bound, all paths and cycles in
G^{\prime}
have size at most
\lambda^{\prime-1/3}
w.h.p.
By Definition
2.3
and the fact that
G^{\prime}
is a collection of paths and cycles (Lemma
A.2
), the conductance
\phi_{G^{\prime}}
of
G^{\prime}
is at least
2\lambda^{\prime 1/3}
.
By Cheeger’s inequality, w.h.p. the spectral gap of
G^{\prime}
is at least
{\phi_{G^{\prime}}}^{2}/2\geq\lambda^{\prime 2/3}\geq\lambda^{\prime}
.

Finally, by our assumption,
ALG
computes the connected components of
G^{\prime}
in
o(\log(1/\lambda^{\prime}))
rounds w.h.p., giving the lemma.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

If
G
has
m
edges at the beginning of a round, then w.h.p.
G
has at most
\max\{3^{\sqrt{\log n}},m\lambda^{\prime 1/5}\}
edges at the end of that round.

Proof.

Observe that after
ALG
contracts all edges of
G^{\prime}
in this round, the resulting edges in
G
are exactly those deleted edges in this round.
The expected number of deleted edges is
m\lambda^{\prime 1/4}
.
If the number of deleted edges is at most
3^{\sqrt{\log n}}
, then the lemma holds.
Otherwise, by a Chernoff bound, the number of deleted edges is at most
2m\lambda^{\prime 1/4}
w.p. at least

1-\exp\left(-m\lambda^{\prime 1/4}/3\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(-3^{\sqrt{\log n}}/6\right)\geq 1-n^{-9},

where the second inequality follows from the fact that
2m\lambda^{\prime 1/4}\geq 3^{\sqrt{\log n}}
.
The lemma follows from
2m\lambda^{\prime 1/4}\leq m\lambda^{\prime 1/5}
.
∎

Now set parameter
R=-10\log n/\log\lambda^{\prime}
.
(Recall that
R
is the number of rounds of the algorithm.)
Initially the graph
G
has at most
n^{2}
edges.
Applying Lemma
A.4
for
R
times, we have that the number of edges at the end of round
R
is at most

3^{\sqrt{\log n}}+n^{2}\cdot(\lambda^{\prime 1/5})^{R}\leq 3^{\sqrt{\log n}}+n^{2}\cdot\lambda^{\prime-2\log n/\log\lambda^{\prime}}\leq 3^{\sqrt{\log n}}+1=n^{o(1)}

w.h.p. by a union bound over all
R\leq\poly\log(n)
rounds. (We use the fact that the spectral gap
\lambda^{\prime}\geq 1/\poly(n)
.)

By Lemma
A.3
, the total number of rounds are at most

R\cdot o(\log(1/\lambda^{\prime}))=o(\log(1/\lambda^{\prime})\cdot(-10\log n/\log\lambda^{\prime}))=o(\log n)

w.h.p., giving an MPC algorithm that distinguishes one cycle of size
n
with two cycles of size
n/2
in
o(\log n)
rounds, a contradiction.

Appendix B
Edge Sampling Does Not Preserve Diameter

In this section we show that edge sampling does not preserve the diameter of graphs, which partially explains why an
O(\log d)
-time, linear-work PRAM algorithm is hard to obtain.

We provide an undirected graph
G
with a diameter of
O(\log n)
such that if we sample every edge in
G
with probability of
p=\frac{1}{\log n}
, the largest diameter in the connected components of
G
would be at least
\frac{n}{\poly(\log n)}
where
n
is the number of vertices in the graph.
This shows that edge sampling can dramatically increase the diameter of a graph.

We construct the graph
G=(V,E)
as follows. The vertex set of
G
consists of
V=(\{x\}\cup P\cup Z)
. Here,
Z
consists of the vertices
\{z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{k}\}
where
k
is a parameter determined later. Also,
P=P_{1}\cup P_{2}\cup\cdots\cup P_{k}
where each
P_{i}
is a vertex-disjoint path of length
\log n
between
x
and
z_{i}
. In the graph
G
we further add an edge between any two vertices
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
such that
|i-j|\leq(\log n)^{3}
.
Note that in the mentioned construction, we have
n=|V|=1+k+k\log n
. This implies
k=\frac{n-1}{\log n+1}
.

It is clear that the graph
G
has a diameter of at most
2\log n
since every vertex is within distance of at most
\log n
from
x
. Now let
\widetilde{G}
be a graph with the same vertex set as
G
, and we add every edge in
G
to
\widetilde{G}
independently with the probability
p=\frac{1}{\log n}
. We claim that w.h.p. the largest diameter in
\widetilde{G}
is
\Omega(\frac{n}{\poly(\log n)})
. First notice that w.h.p.
x
is not connected to any of the vertices
z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{k}
.
This is because there was a path
P_{i}
with length
\log n
between
x
and each
z_{i}
in
G
. The probability that we sample all of the edges of
P_{i}
and include them in
\widetilde{G}
is
(\frac{1}{\log n})^{\log n}
which is at most
1/{n^{3}}
for large enough
n
. Thus, with probability at least
1-1/n^{3}
,
x
is not connected to
z_{i}
via the path
P_{i}
. By taking the union bound, we can say that with probability at least
1-1/n^{2}
,
x
is not connected to any of the vertex in
Z
.

Now we show that w.h.p. the subgraph induced by the vertices of
Z
remains connected. This immediately implies that the diameter in connected component of the vertices of
Z
is at least
\Omega(\frac{n}{\poly(\log n)})
. Consider the shortest path between
z_{1}
and
z_{k}
. Since none of the vertices
z_{i}
are connected to
x
, the shortest way to travel from
z_{1}
and
z_{k}
is by using the vertices in
Z
. Recall that each
z_{i}
is connected to
z_{j}
in
G
iff
|i-j|\leq\log^{2}n
. Thus, in order to travel from
z_{1}
to
z_{k}
we have to use at least
\Omega(\frac{k}{(\log n)^{3}})=\Omega(\frac{n}{\poly(\log n)})
edges. Thus, the diameter in the connected component of the vertices of
Z
is at least
\Omega(\frac{n}{\poly(\log n)})
. It is now sufficient to show that w.h.p. the subgraph induced by the vertices of
Z
remains connected in
\widetilde{G}
.

To show the connectivity, first consider the vertices
z_{1},\cdots,z_{(\log n)^{3}}
. We show that these vertices form a connected subgraph in
\widetilde{G}
. Notice that these vertices form a complete induced subgraph in
G
. Since we sample each edge with probability
p=1/\log n
, we can say that in
\widetilde{G}
, the subgraph induced by these vertices has the same distribution as
G((\log n)^{3},p)
. Here,
G(n,p)
is used to denote the Erdős–Rényi graph with
n
vertices where each edge is included in it independently with probability
p
.
It is known that
G(n,p)
is connected with probability
e^{-e^{-c}}
if
p\geq\frac{(1+c)\ln n}{n}

[
ER+60
]
.
Recall that in the random subgraph
G((\log n)^{3},p)
, we have
p=1/\log n
.
Thus,
p\gg\frac{3\ln n\ln((\log n)^{3})}{(\log n)^{3}}
for large enough
n
which implies the connectivity of this random graph w.p.
1-1/n^{2}
.

So far, we have showed that w.h.p. the induced subgraph of the first
(\log n)^{3}
vertices of
Z
is connected.
Now consider a vertex
z_{i}
where
i>(\log n)^{3}
, we show that w.h.p.
z_{i}
has an edge in
\widetilde{G}
to some other vertex
z_{j}
where
j<i
.
Recall that there is an edge between
z_{i}
and each of the vertices
z_{i-1},\cdots,z_{i-(\log n)^{3}}
in
G
.
Thus, the probability that we include none of these edges in
\widetilde{G}
is
(1-1/\log n)^{(\log n)^{3}}
which is at most
1/n^{3}
for large enough
n
. Therefore, we can say that with probability at least
1-1/n^{3}
there is an edge between
z_{i}
and another vertex
z_{j}
where
j<i
. By taking the union bound, we can say that w.h.p. for every vertex
z_{i}
where
i>(\log n)^{3}
, there is an edge between
z_{i}
and some other vertex
z_{j}
where
j<i
. This is enough to show that the induced subgraph of vertices
Z
is connected.
To see that, we show w.h.p. there is a path from every vertex
z_{i}
to
z_{1}
in
\widetilde{G}
. Consider a vertex
z_{i}
, if
i>(\log n)^{3}
, we know that there is an edge between
z_{i}
and some other vertex
z_{j}
where
j<i
. Thus, we can travel from
z_{i}
to
z_{j}
as long as
i>(\log n)^{3}
. By traveling to these neighbors repeatedly, we eventually reach a vertex
z_{j}
such that
j\leq(\log n)^{3}
. In this case we already know that the induced subgraph of the first
(\log n)^{3}
vertices of
Z
is connected. Thus, there is a path from
z_{j}
to
z_{1}
in
\widetilde{G}
which implies that there is also a path from
z_{i}
to
z_{1}
.

Appendix C
Spectrum of Randomly Sampled Graphs

We prove a concentration bound for the spectrum of randomly sampled graphs.
Our proof is an adaptation of that of Oliveira
[
Oli10
]
. Specifically, known concentration bounds
for the spectrum of randomly sampled graphs
[
CH07
,
Oli10
,
CR11
]

are stated for graphs without parallel edges.
In our application, we need essentially the same concentration
bounds for multi-graphs, i.e., allowing parallel edges and loops.
It is not too difficult to extend the proof of Oliveira
[
Oli10
]
to multi-graphs. For completeness, we provide the
modified proof in this section.

C.1
Preliminaries

Suppose
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a vector over the reals, we use
the notation
\|x\|
to denote the Euclidean norm of
x
.

Definition C.1

(Spectral radius norm)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a symmetric matrix over the reals.
We define the spectral radius norm of
A
, denoted
\|A\|
,
to be the following:

\|A\|=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|x\|=1}\|Ax\|

Let
\lambda_{0}(A)\leq\lambda_{1}(A)\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{n-1}(A)
be the
eigenvalues of
A
, which correspond to orthonormal eigenvectors

\psi_{0},\ldots,\psi_{n-1}
.
The
spectrum
of
A
is the set of all eigenvalues of
A
.
Since
A
is symmetric, the following holds:

\|A\|=\max_{0\leq i\leq n-1}|\lambda_{i}(A)|

Theorem 5

(Matrix concentration bound
[
Oli10
,
CR11
]
)
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}
be mean-zero independent
n\times n

random matrices over the reals.
Suppose that there exists a
B>0
such that
for all
1\leq i\leq m
,

\|X_{i}\|\leq B
; and let
\sigma^{2}=\lambda_{\rm max}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\E[X_{i}^{2}]\right)
.
Then, for all
t\geq 0
,

\Pr\left(\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}\right\|\geq t\right)\leq 2n\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}+4Bt}\right)

C.2
Spectrum of Randomly Sampled Graphs

Consider a graph
\widetilde{G}
that is down-sampled from
some original graph
G
through the following process:

•

\widetilde{G}
has the same set of vertices as
G
;

•

for every edge (including loops and parallel edges)
in
G
,
with probability
p\in(0,1)
, preserve the edge in
\widetilde{G}
.

We want to show that the down-sampled graph

\widetilde{G}
approximately preserves the spectrum
of the original graph
G
, as
stated in the following theorem:

Theorem 6

(Spectrum of randomly sampled graph)
.

Let
\deg(G)
be the minimum degree of any vertice in
G
,
let
n
be the number of vertices in
G
, and let
N\geq n
.
For any constant
c>0
, there exists
a constant
C=C(c)>0
,
such that
if
p\cdot\deg(G)\geq C\cdot\ln N
, then
for
N^{-c}\leq\delta\leq 1/2
,

\Pr\left(\|\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{G})-\mathcal{L}(G)\|\leq 13\sqrt{\frac{\ln(\frac{4n}{\delta})}{p\cdot\deg(G)}}\right)\geq 1-\delta

Proof.

The expected degree of every vertex
v
in
\widetilde{G}
is at least

\E[d_{\widetilde{G}}(v)]=p\cdot d_{G}(v)\geq p\cdot\deg(G)
. By the standard Chernoff bound,
for a sufficiently large constant
C
, we have

\forall\text{vertex }v:\Pr\left(\left|\frac{d_{\widetilde{G}}(v)}{p\cdot d_{G}(v)}-1\right|>2\sqrt{\frac{\ln\frac{4n}{\delta}}{p\cdot d_{G}(v)}}\right)\leq\frac{\delta}{2n}

By the union bound,
with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,
we have that

\forall\text{vertex }v\in[n]:\left|\frac{d_{\widetilde{G}}(v)}{p\cdot d_{G}(v)}-1\right|\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{\ln\frac{4n}{\delta}}{p\cdot d_{G}(v)}}

Since
\delta\geq N^{-c}
,
for a sufficiently large constant
C
, the above implies that
with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

\forall\text{vertex }v\in[n]:\left|\frac{p\cdot d_{G}(v)}{d_{\widetilde{G}}(v)}-1\right|\leq 4\sqrt{\frac{\ln\frac{4n}{\delta}}{p\cdot d_{G}(v)}}\leq 4\sqrt{\frac{\ln\frac{4n}{\delta}}{p\cdot\deg(G)}}

(8)

We will the above inequality to compare the matrices:

\widetilde{T}=\text{diagonal with }d_{\widetilde{G}}(v)^{-1/2}\text{ at the $(v,v)$-th position}

and

T=\text{diagonal with }(p\cdot d_{G}(v))^{-1/2}\text{ at the $(v,v)$-th position}

For a sufficiently large constant
C
, the right-hand-side
of Equation (
8
) is at most
3/4
.
Observe that for any
x\in[-3/4,3/4]
,
it must be

\left|\sqrt{1+x}-1\right|\leq x
.
Applying this fact to
x=\frac{p\cdot d_{G}(v)}{d_{\widetilde{G}}(v)}-1
,
we have the following with probability
at least
1-\delta/2
:

\|T\widetilde{T}^{-1}-I\|=\max_{1\leq v\leq n}\left|\frac{\sqrt{p\cdot d_{G}(v)}}{\sqrt{d_{\widetilde{G}}(v)}}-1\right|\leq 4\sqrt{\frac{\ln(4n/\delta)}{p\cdot\deg(G)}}

(9)

Let
A=p\cdot A(G)
where
A(G)
is the adjacency matrix
of
G
, and let
\widetilde{A}=A(\widetilde{G})
be the adjacency matrix
of the sampled graph
\widetilde{G}
.
Recall that for a multi-graph, the

(u,v)
-position in the adjacency matrix stores the
number of edges between
u
and
v
.
We now compare
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}=I-\widetilde{T}\widetilde{A}\widetilde{T}

and
\mathcal{L}=I-TAT
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}

is the normalized Laplacian of
\widetilde{G}
and
\mathcal{L}
is the normalized
Laplacian of
G
.
To compare the two, we will introduce
an intermediate matrix

\mathcal{M}=I-T\widetilde{A}T=I-(T\widetilde{T}^{-1})(I-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})(T\widetilde{T}^{-1})

We have that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\|

\displaystyle=\|(T\widetilde{T}^{-1})(I-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})(T\widetilde{T}^{-1})-(I-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})\|

\displaystyle\leq\|(T\widetilde{T}^{-1}-I)(I-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})(T\widetilde{T}^{-1})-(I-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})(T\widetilde{T}^{-1}-I)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|T\widetilde{T}^{-1}-I\|\cdot\|I-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\|\cdot\|T\widetilde{T}^{-1}\|+\|I-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\|\cdot\|T\widetilde{T}^{-1}-I\|

Now, since the spectrum of any normalized Laplacian matrix lies
in
[0,2]
, it must be that
\|I-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\|\leq 1
.
Using this fact as well as
Equation (
9
), we have that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\|\leq 4\sqrt{\frac{\ln(4n/\delta)}{p\cdot\deg(G)}}\cdot\left(1+4\sqrt{\frac{\ln(4n/\delta)}{p\cdot\deg(G)}}\right)+4\sqrt{\frac{\ln(4n/\delta)}{p\cdot\deg(G)}}\leq 9\sqrt{\frac{\ln(4n/\delta)}{p\cdot\deg(G)}}

where the last inequality again relies
on the fact that
C
is a sufficiently large constant.

To finish the proof, it suffices
to show that
\|\mathcal{M}-\mathcal{L}\|\leq 4\sqrt{\frac{\ln(4n/\delta)}{p\cdot\deg(G)}}

with probability at least
1-\delta/2
.
Let
e=(u,v)
be an edge in
G
,
we define the matrix
A_{e}
as the adjacency matrix
for a single edge
e
:

A_{e}=\begin{cases}{\bf b}_{u}{\bf b}_{v}^{T}+{\bf b}_{v}{\bf b}_{u}^{T}&\text{if $u\neq v$}\\
{\bf b}_{u}{\bf b}_{u}^{T}&\text{if $u=v$}\end{cases}

where
{\bf b}_{u}

denote the
u
-th vector in the standard basis.

Henceforth,
for every edge
e\in G
,
let
I_{e}

be the indicator random variable that denotes
whether the edge
e
is preserved in the down-sampled graph
\widetilde{G}
;
define the random matrix
X_{e}=(I_{e}-p)\cdot A_{e}
.
We can rewrite
\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{M}
as the following
where the summation over
e\in G
iterates over all
parallel edges (including loops) with the corresponding multiplicity:

\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{M}=\sum_{e\in G}TX_{e}T

Observe that each
X_{e}
is an independent mean-0 matrix.
Let
Y_{e}=TX_{e}T
, we have the following
where we use
u_{e},v_{e}
to iterate
over the two vertices
incident to the edge
e
:

Y_{e}=T\cdot(I_{e}-p)\cdot A_{e}\cdot T=(I_{e}-p)\cdot\frac{A_{e}}{p\cdot\sqrt{d_{G}(u_{e})\cdot{d_{G}(v_{e})}}}

The possible eigenvalues of
Y_{e}

are contained in the following set:

\left\{\frac{\pm\left(1-p\right)}{p\cdot\sqrt{d_{G}(u_{e})\cdot{d_{G}(v_{e})}}},\frac{\pm p}{p\cdot\sqrt{d_{G}(u_{e})\cdot{d_{G}(v_{e})}}},0\right\}

Therefore,

\|Y_{e}\|\leq\frac{1}{p\cdot\sqrt{d_{G}(u_{e})\cdot{d_{G}(v_{e})}}}\leq\frac{1}{p\cdot\deg(G)}

The sum of variance is the following
where the notation
\sum_{e=(u,v)\in G}

iterates over all edges adjacent to some fixed
u
in
G
:

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in G}\E[Y_{e}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in G}\E[(I_{e}-p)^{2}]\cdot\left(\frac{A_{e}}{p\cdot\sqrt{d_{G}(u_{e})\cdot{d_{G}(v_{e})}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(u,v)\in G,u\neq v}p(1-p)\cdot\frac{{\bf b}_{u}{\bf b}_{u}^{T}+{\bf b}_{v}{\bf b}_{v}^{T}}{p^{2}\cdot d_{G}(u)\cdot d_{G}(v)}+\sum_{(u,u)\in G}p(1-p)\cdot\frac{{\bf b}_{u}{\bf b}_{u}^{T}}{p^{2}\cdot d_{G}(u)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-p}{p}\cdot\sum_{u\in G}\frac{1}{d_{G}(u)}\sum_{e=(u,v)\in G}\frac{{\bf b}_{u}{\bf b}_{u}^{T}}{d_{G}(v)}

The above is a diagonal matrix, and its
(u,u)
-th entry
is at most:

\displaystyle\frac{1-p}{p}\cdot\frac{1}{d_{G}(u)}\cdot\sum_{e=(u,v)\in G}\frac{1}{d_{G}(v)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-p}{p}\cdot\frac{1}{d_{G}(u)}\cdot\sum_{e=(u,v)\in G}\frac{1}{\deg(G)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-p}{p}\cdot\frac{1}{d_{G}(u)}\cdot d_{G}(u)\cdot\frac{1}{\deg(G)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p\cdot\deg(G)}

Now, we apply the matrix concentration theorem, i.e.,
Theorem
5
,
to
\sum_{e}Y_{e}

with
B=\sigma^{2}=\frac{1}{p\cdot\deg(G)}
.
We thus have

\Pr\left(\|\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{M}\|\geq t\right)\leq 2n\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}\cdot p\cdot\deg(G)}{8+4t}\right)

Plugging in
t=4\sqrt{\frac{\ln(4n/\delta)}{p\cdot\deg(G)}}\leq 3/4
,
we have

\Pr\left(\|\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{M}\|\geq 4\sqrt{\frac{\ln(4n/\delta)}{p\cdot\deg(G)}}\right)\leq 2n\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{16\ln(4n/\delta)}{16}\right)\leq\delta/2

∎

Corollary C.2

(Random sampling approximately preserves spectral gap)
.

Let
n
be the number of vertices of
G
and let
N\geq n
.
Let
c>0
be an arbitrary constant.
There exist constants
C

and
C^{\prime}
such that as long as
p\cdot\deg(G)\geq C\ln N
,
then with probability at least
1-1/N^{c}
,

|\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{L})-\lambda_{1}(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})|\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\ln n}{p\cdot\deg(G)}}

where
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}(G)

and
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{G})

denote the normalized Laplacian matrices of the original graph
G

and the sampled graph
\widetilde{G}
, respectively.

Proof.

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})=\min_{{\bf 1}^{T}x=0,\|x\|=1}x^{T}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}x

\displaystyle=\min_{{\bf 1}^{T}x=0,\|x\|=1}x^{T}\left(\mathcal{L}+(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}-\mathcal{L})\right)x

\displaystyle\geq\min_{{\bf 1}^{T}y=0,\|y\|=1}y^{T}\mathcal{L}y-\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}-\mathcal{L}\|

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}({\mathcal{L}})-\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}-\mathcal{L}\|

Similarly, we can show the other direction, that is,

\lambda_{1}({\mathcal{L}})\geq\lambda_{1}(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}})-\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}-\mathcal{L}\|
.
The corollary now follows in a straightforward manner
from Theorem
6
.
∎

Corollary C.3

(Random sampling approximately preserves the spectral gap of
every connected component)
.

Suppose that the graph
G
contains
k
connected components
denoted
G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}
.
Let
N
be the number of vertices of
G
. Let
c>0
be an arbitrary constant.
There exist constants
C

and
C^{\prime}
such that as long as
p\cdot\deg(G)\geq C\ln N
,
then with probability at least
1-1/N^{c}
,
it must be that for all
i\in[k]
,

|\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{L}(G_{i}))-\lambda_{1}({\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{G_{i}})})|\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\ln N}{p\cdot\deg(G)}}

where
\mathcal{L}(G)

and
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{G})

denote the normalized Laplacian matrices of the original connected component
G_{i}

and the connected component
\widetilde{G}_{i}
after sampling, respectively.

Proof.

Follows in a straightforward fashion
from
Corollary
C.2

by taking a union bound over all the at most
N
connected components.
∎
